2. Key Technical Concepts:
- **Simulation setup:** LAMMPS + DeePMD (deepmd_py311, graph_c.pb; same model md5 81f3fc48 at both volumes).
  - Oscillatory NEMD uses fix deform wiggle with nvt/sllod and lock-in analysis.
  - GK uses NVE stress autocorrelations.
- **Corrections and protocol rules:**
  - Lag correction: exp(iωΔt).
  - Q handling: liquid-like points and 2σ bounds.
  - Uniaxial thermostat rule: large τ_T (adiabatic).
  - Bicrystal slab resonance: f₁ ≈ v_s/L_z.
  - Schottky cells age; check PE stationarity.
  - Crystallization can happen during the NVE GK run (cut the analysis with GKCUT).
- **V9.6397 cell:** L = 34.051872 Å, B1 a = 4.256484 Å.
- **RC's old V9.6397 NEMD runs:**

| T (K) | p (GPa) | state |
|---|---|---|
| 8000 | ≈ 60 | liquid |
| 6000 | ≈ 50 | liquid |
| 5000 | ≈ 44 | liquid |
| 4000 | ≈ 38 | liquid, 1 ns, etot −18260 eV |
| 3000 | ≈ 18 | crystallized |

- **Speed at V9.6397:** about 32 steps/s (V6.6868: 24), so 1.5 ns GK ≈ 13 h. Total V9 cost ≈ 25.6 M steps ≈ 220 P100-h ≈ 9 P100-days.
- **Trajectory dumps:** `id type x y z ix iy iz`, about 250 kB per frame; view in OVITO with the displacement or atomic-strain modifiers.

**/resnick/home/recohen/Claude/tools/sweep_array.sh** (new, generic P100 array job)
- Reads the tasks.txt columns `D TEMP PERIOD AMP NTOT NCHUNK NAV SEED NEQ MAXCHUNKS MODE TDAMP [DT DUMPN DUMPSTEPS DATA]`.

- `mkdir -p $ROOT/$D; cd $ROOT/$D`.

### Claude (10-08 06:56)

The documentation is written, the V9.6397 runs are underway, and sample trajectories are running at the original volume (V6.6868). First, a correction: the aged-Schottky job I reported as queued last time (4230009) had actually failed within seconds. The file-conversion step crashed because it set no interatomic potential. I fixed that and resubmitted it as 4233462, and all three tasks are now running.

**Documentation**
- `ANELASTICITY_METHODS.md` is a standalone guide for a future session or another material. It covers:
  - the physics, with numbered equations (lock-in, Q, Green–Kubo, the one-step lag correction, the thermostat artefact, heating, slab resonance);
  - the protocol that worked, every tool and how to call it, job and environment rules, measured speeds and costs, and all the pitfalls found;
  - a porting checklist. For example, atomic masses are hard-coded in the LAMMPS inputs, and the defect builder only does rock-salt.
- `ANELASTICITY_RESTART_2026-10-08.md` lists the job IDs and what to check first. TODO, DONE and memory are updated.
- The collection script no longer has V6.6868 hard-coded. Each volume or material gets its own case file (`anelastic_cases_MgO_V6.6868.py` and `..._V9.6397.py`). The V6.6868 output is unchanged.

**Trajectories**
- The sweep input now takes `DUMPN`/`DUMPSTEPS`, which save a trajectory only for the first part of a run.
- Eight sample runs at V6.6868 are going (job 4233485), each 3–5 cycles and about 75 MB:
  - liquid 8000 K: shear at 20 ps and 1 ps periods, and uniaxial at 5 ps;
  - 5000 K crystals at a 20 ps period: perfect B1, aged Schottky and bicrystal;
  - two extra bicrystal runs: one at γ₀ = 0.1 so the boundary sliding is visible by eye, and one at the 1 ps resonance.
- Two have finished and contain 501 frames each.
- In OVITO, the solid strains (0.01–0.03) are too small to see directly. Show them with displacement vectors scaled up, or with atomic strain.

**V9.6397 runs**
Your earlier runs there show liquid down to 4000 K (≈ 38 GPa) and crystallization at 3000 K (≈ 18 GPa). The same DeePMD model was used, so the compressed version is reused. I submitted your choice:
- **Green–Kubo runs** for the 6000 and 4000 K liquids, the 3000 K melt-grown crystal, and the perfect, Schottky and bicrystal cells at 3000 K (jobs 4233549–54).
- **Starting states:** your `Liq8000.in.pos`, and your 4000 K `end.pos` for both the 4000 K run and the 3000 K melt-grown run. The melt-grown run gets 300 ps of NVT to crystallize.
- **Sweeps** submit automatically as each run finishes equilibrating. Strains are the same as at V6.6868, with amplitudes scaled to the larger box. The low-frequency 20 and 50 ps defect runs are included, and the 20 ps shear tasks save trajectories.
- **Waiting on a check:** the 3000 K melt-grown sweep and the 2000 K run, which starts from the 3000 K crystal. I'll confirm the crystal formed (energy drop and plateau, Bragg peaks) before launching them.
- **Cost:** it runs at about 32 steps/s here versus 24 at V6.6868, so the total is about 9 P100-days instead of 12. Each 1.5 ns Green–Kubo run takes about 13 h.

**Bicrystal cell fix:** the bicrystal builder's overlap cutoff didn't scale with the lattice constant, which left atoms 1.35 Å apart at this volume. It now scales, and the rebuilt cell has the same 3920-atom structure as at V6.6868.

```
7:Moduli from the lock-in fit (sweep) or Green–Kubo (GK, NVE). Shear: $G^*$. Uniaxial x: $M^*=K^*+\frac43G^*$.
9:$$G''(\omega)=\eta_0\,\omega+O(\omega^3)\;\Rightarrow\;\eta_0=\lim_{\omega\to0}\frac{dG''}{d\omega} \tag{1}$$
10:$$M''(\omega)=(\zeta_0+\tfrac43\eta_0)\,\omega+O(\omega^3) \tag{2}$$
12:$$\eta_0=\frac{\sum_i w_i\omega_iG''_i}{\sum_i w_i\omega_i^2},\quad w_i=\sigma_i^{-2},\ f_i\le200\ \mathrm{GHz} \tag{4}$$
13:$$\frac{dG''}{d\omega}=\int_0^{t_c}G(t)\left[\cos\omega t-\omega t\sin\omega t\right]dt\ \text{(GK)} \tag{5}$$
14:$$\eta'(\omega)=G''(\omega)/\omega \tag{6}$$
149:| run | points (f ≤ 200 GHz) | $\eta_0$ or $\zeta_0+\frac43\eta_0$ from Eq. 4 (mPa s) |
160:| T target (K) | ⟨T⟩ (K) | ⟨p⟩ (GPa) | run (ps) | $G_\infty$ (GPa) | $\eta_0$ (mPa s) | $\zeta_0$ (mPa s) | $\int M_r$ (mPa s) | $dG''/d\omega$ at 3 GHz (mPa s, Eq. 5) | $\tau_0=\eta_0/G_\infty$ (ps) |
171:Rows 5000, 4000, 5000 B1 ref, 5000 Schottky and 5000 bicrystal are **crystalline** (B1). There, $G(0)=V\langle\delta\sigma^2\rangle/k_BT$ is only the fluctuation part of the modulus (the Born term is missing), and $\int G\,dt$ is the low-frequency loss from phonon damping and defects ($G''\approx\omega\int G\,dt$), not a viscosity. The elastic $G'$ of the solid comes from the sweeps.
173:| T (K) | $\eta(t_c)$ at $t_c$ = 0.5, 1, 2, 3 ps (mPa s) | $\zeta(t_c)$ at the same $t_c$ (mPa s) |
184:| T (K) | f | $G'$ (GPa) | $G''$ (GPa) | $\eta'$ (mPa s) | $dG''/d\omega$ (mPa s) | $M''$ GK (GPa) | $\zeta'$ (mPa s) | $Q=G'/G''$ (liquid, Eq. 8) |
```

```
  dt=np.median(np.diff(a[:,0]))*1e-15; P=a[:,1:7]*1e5; T=a[:,7].mean()
  n=len(P); nl=int(3e-12/dt)+1
  def acf(x):
    x=x-x.mean(); F=np.fft.rfft(x,2*n); return np.fft.irfft(F*np.conj(F))[:nl]/np.arange(n,n-nl,-1)
  off=np.mean([acf(P[:,k]) for k in (3,4,5)],0)
  nd=np.mean([acf((P[:,0]-P[:,1])/2),acf((P[:,1]-P[:,2])/2),acf((P[:,0]-P[:,2])/2)],0)
  t=np.arange(nl)*dt
  for nm,c in (('offdiag C44-type',off),('normal-diff (C11-C12)/2-type',nd)):
    G=V/(kB*T)*c; s=f'{lab:16s} {nm:30s} '
    for f in (0.0,0.2,1.0):
      s+=f' {f}THz:{np.trapz(G*np.cos(2*np.pi*f*1e12*t),t)*1e3:.2f}'
    print(s)
EOF
```

### Claude (10-08 08:32)

The two methods don't really have opposite slopes once you compare them at the same frequency. The mismatch only appears in the crystal, because the plot puts two different quantities next to each other.

**What the plot compares.** In the liquid, Green–Kubo and the sweeps agree, and both rise on cooling (18.3 vs 17.1 mPa s at 6000 K). In the crystal, panel (a) shows Green–Kubo's ∫G dt, which is the loss G″/ω extrapolated to zero frequency. The sweeps measure G″/ω at 0.2–5 THz. The crystal's stress correlations decay over picoseconds because of phonon (Akhiezer-type) relaxation, with relaxation time τ. Those two frequencies sit on opposite sides of the loss peak:
- Below the peak (ωτ ≪ 1), G″/ω ≈ ΔG·τ. This falls with T, because phonon lifetimes get shorter as T rises.
- Above the peak (ωτ ≳ 1), G″/ω ≈ ΔG/(ω²τ). This rises with T.

At 200 GHz with τ of about 1–3 ps, ωτ is about 1–4, so the sweeps are on the side that rises with T.

**Check.** I recomputed the Green–Kubo loss at the sweep frequencies from the stored stress series (t_c = 3 ps), using the same xz-type (off-diagonal) stress components the sweep drives:

| crystal | f | GK η′ (mPa s) | sweep η′ (mPa s) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4000 K melt-grown | ω → 0 | 10.1 (not converged) | — |
| | 0.2 THz | 4.26 | 3.71 ± 0.73 |
| | 1 THz | 1.73 | 1.76 ± 0.18 |
| 5000 K melt-grown | ω → 0 | 6.25 | — |
| | 0.2 THz | 4.87 | 6.67 ± 1.45 |
| | 1 THz | 2.34 | 2.29 ± 0.06 |
| 5000 K perfect B1 | 0.2 THz | 0.51 | 0.13 ± 0.37 |
| | 1 THz | 0.22 | 0.08 ± 0.08 |

At the same frequency the two methods agree, and both rise from 4000 to 5000 K at 1 THz. Only the zero-frequency value falls with T. The 4000 K zero-frequency integral was still rising at 3 ps, so the gap is real and is probably even larger.

**A second problem this exposed.** The plotted Green–Kubo "η₀" averages all five shear stress components. In a cube-aligned crystal those are two different shears:
- **Off-diagonal stresses (C₄₄-type):** this is what the xz sweep drives. In perfect B1 it is almost lossless: 0.74 mPa s at ω → 0.
- **Normal-stress differences ((C₁₁−C₁₂)/2-type):** this one is lossy: 7.8 mPa s.

That is why perfect B1 showed a Green–Kubo value of about 3.4 mPa s while its sweep loss was essentially zero. The melt-grown crystal has an arbitrary orientation, so it mixes the two shears.

**Proposed fix.** In the crystal panels, plot Green–Kubo η′ at the sweep frequencies, split into C₄₄-type and (C₁₁−C₁₂)/2-type. Relabel the zero-frequency integral as the "relaxed phonon loss" rather than η₀, and add this as a pitfall in the methods doc. Should I make that change to the collection script now? It would also apply to the V9.6397 results.

## User (10-08 08:54)

I was looking at the line for P!

### Claude (10-08 08:55)

Sorry, I misread which line you meant. The grey line with open diamonds is the pressure on the right axis, not a viscosity from the sweeps (NEMD). It is the mean pressure ⟨p⟩ from the same Green–Kubo runs. It rises with T for a simple reason: every run is at the same volume (6.6868 Å³/atom), so heating raises the pressure.

**Why it goes up with T.** At fixed volume the slope is (∂p/∂T)_V = α·K_T, the thermal expansion coefficient times the bulk modulus. From the run averages:

| range | Δp (GPa) | slope (GPa per 1000 K) |
|---|---|---|
| liquid, 6000 → 8000 K | 174.9 → 192.8 | ≈ 9 |
| crystal, 4000 → 5000 K | 144.9 → 151.8 | ≈ 7 |

**The jump between 5000 and 6000 K** is melting at fixed volume. At the same volume and temperature, the liquid has a higher pressure than the crystal. Extending the liquid line down to 5000 K gives about 166 GPa, against 152 GPa for the crystal, so the offset is about 14 GPa.

**Viscosity versus pressure.** The viscosity falls with T while the pressure rises because these are fixed-volume runs. The density does not change, and temperature has the stronger effect. Along a fixed-pressure path the viscosity would fall even more steeply, because pressure would also drop as the liquid expanded.

To make this clearer in the figure, I could draw the pressure as a dashed grey line labelled "pressure (right axis)" so it isn't mistaken for a data series. My earlier answer still stands as a separate issue: the crystal-phase Green–Kubo value in panel (a) is a zero-frequency loss, not η₀, and it mixes two kinds of shear. Should I fix both in the plotting script?

## User (10-08 08:56)

So when you go from liquid to crystal viscosity should skyrocket. Instead it seems to go down?

### Claude (10-08 08:56)

You're right that it should, and it does. The open circles in panel (a) are not a viscosity. They measure phonon damping, and putting them on the same axis as the liquid η₀ is misleading. That's my fault in the plot.

**Why Green–Kubo gives a small number in the crystal.** Viscosity is the integral of the full stress relaxation function, η = ∫G(t) dt.
- In a liquid, G(t) decays to zero, so the integral is finite.
- In a crystal, G(t) levels off at the static shear modulus, about 270 GPa, so the integral is infinite: the crystal does not flow on MD time scales.
- Green–Kubo only sees stress fluctuations at fixed cell shape, so it drops that static part. What is left is the damping of thermal phonons, the internal friction in the circles. Its natural unit is mPa s, but it is not flow.

**The jump is in the sweeps, in the modulus.** At the lowest frequencies:

| | G′ (GPa) | Q |
|---|---|---|
| 6000 K liquid, 10 GHz | ≈ 0.01 | ≈ 0.01 |
| 5000 K crystal, 200 GHz | 258 | 31 |
| 4000 K crystal, 200 GHz | 278 | 60 |

The liquid has no shear stiffness at low frequency; the crystal keeps all of it. That is the solid behaviour you expected.

**A lower bound on the crystal's viscosity.** If the crystal could flow at all, the largest flow viscosity consistent with no visible relaxation follows from the Maxwell form. There τ = Q/ω, and η = G′·τ = G′·Q/ω, at ω = 2π × 200 GHz:
- 4000 K: η ≳ 278 GPa × 60 / 1.26×10¹² s⁻¹ ≈ 13 Pa s.
- 5000 K: η ≳ 258 GPa × 31 / 1.26×10¹² s⁻¹ ≈ 6 Pa s.

These are bounds set by how long the runs are. The liquid at 6000 K is 0.018 Pa s, so the crystal is already at least 300–700 times more viscous. Real creep viscosity, from dislocations and diffusion, is many orders of magnitude higher and out of reach for MD.

**Proposed figure fix.** In panel (a), replace the open circles with upward arrows at η > G′Q/ω, which shows the jump at melting. Move the phonon-damping values to their own panel or table, labelled "internal friction (not viscosity)", and plot them at the sweep frequencies, separated by type of shear as above. Also draw the pressure line dashed. Should I go ahead? The same change would apply to the V9.6397 figures.

### Claude (10-08 09:00)

I've sent you `fig_effective_viscosity` (PNG and PDF). Panel (b) is the log plot of viscosity versus temperature, and the crystal is now 1–3 orders of magnitude above the liquid, as you expected.

**Panel (b), liquid versus crystal** (log y-axis). The solid symbols are the effective viscosity |η*| = |G*|/ω at the same frequency (200 GHz) for every case. The arrows are lower bounds on how viscous the crystal would be if it flowed: η₀ ≥ G′·Q/ω. This bound assumes the crystal loses stress no faster than a Maxwell fluid would, and uses the 2σ upper error on G″.

| case | |η*| at 200 GHz (mPa s) | flow η₀ lower bound (mPa s) |
|---|---|---|
| liquid 8000 K | 8.0 | (η₀ = 8.2 from Green–Kubo) |
| liquid 7000 K | 11.5 | (11.2) |
| liquid 6000 K | 17.7 | (18.3) |
| crystal 5000 K, melt-grown | 205 | ≥ 4,400 |
| crystal 4000 K, melt-grown | 221 | ≥ 9,400 |
| perfect B1, 5000 K | 136 | ≥ 21,000 |
| Schottky, 5000 K | 118 | ≥ 850 |
| bicrystal, 5000 K | 110 | ≥ 1,700 |

The bounds are limited by how low in frequency the sweeps go, so the real flow viscosity is higher still. The defect cells have the lowest bounds because they relax partly at low frequency.

**Panel (a), |η*| versus frequency.** The liquids level off at η₀ at low frequency. The crystals follow G′/ω, a line of slope −1 that keeps rising as frequency drops. That is how an elastic solid looks in these units: its effective viscosity has no upper limit. Schottky and bicrystal fall slightly below that line at 20–50 GHz, which is their partial relaxation.

The script is `tools/plot_effective_viscosity.py`. It reads `results_all.csv`, so it will also work for V9.6397 once those results are collected. I haven't yet removed the misleading Green–Kubo circles from `fig_vs_T`. I'll make that change, add this figure to the collection script, and add the pitfall to the methods doc unless you'd prefer otherwise.

## User (10-08 09:03)

Very interesting--what are the points with the up arrows. I am surprised that the schottky bicrystal and B1 ref are similar. And their viscosities for solids seem low.

### Claude (10-08 09:03)

The up arrows are lower limits on the crystal's real (zero-frequency) flow viscosity, not measurements. Each comes from the single 200 GHz point, as η₀ ≥ G′·Q/ω. This is the most viscous flow consistent with seeing almost no relaxation at that frequency. The true value is higher, possibly far higher.

**Why perfect B1, Schottky and bicrystal look similar.** The filled symbols at 200 GHz are |η*| ≈ G′/ω for every crystal. At 200 GHz that number is set entirely by stiffness, not by dissipation. A spring driven at frequency ω has a "viscosity" of G′/ω, so the filled points just plot the shear modulus:

| cell | G′ at 200 GHz (GPa) | Q at 200 GHz | flow-η₀ bound (mPa s) |
|---|---|---|---|
| melt-grown 5000 K | 258 | 31 | ≥ 4,400 |
| perfect B1 5000 K | 171 | > 155 | ≥ 21,000 |
| Schottky 5000 K | 148 | 10 | ≥ 850 |
| bicrystal 5000 K | 138 | 21 | ≥ 1,700 |

The perfect, Schottky and bicrystal cells are aligned with the cube axes, so xz shear measures C₄₄ (≈ 170 GPa). Vacancies and boundaries soften it a little, to 148 and 138 GPa. The melt-grown crystal has a random orientation, which mixes in the stiffer (C₁₁−C₁₂)/2 shear, hence 258 GPa.

The real differences between the defects are in the dissipation, and those are large. Q goes from above 155 in the perfect crystal to 10 with Schottky defects. That is why the arrows spread over a factor of 25 while the filled points barely differ.

**Correction to my last message.** I said Schottky and bicrystal fall below the elastic line at 20–50 GHz because of partial relaxation. That was mostly wrong. They lie below the 270 GPa line because their own G′ is only about 140 GPa, for the orientation reason above. Within the error bars, Schottky G′ is flat from 20 to 200 GHz. Only the bicrystal at 20 GHz (108 ± 15 GPa) hints at softening, and it is not significant. The long γ₀ = 0.03 bicrystal runs now going will test it.

**Why the solid viscosities look low.** This comes from the method, not the material. Any test at frequency ω can only show η ≳ G·Q/ω, roughly the modulus times the observation time.
- Our lowest frequencies, 20–200 GHz (5–50 ps periods), cap the bound at about 1–20 Pa s.
- Real creep viscosity of MgO in the mantle is about 10¹⁸–10²² Pa s.
- Seeing that directly would need t ≈ η/G ≈ 10²⁰ Pa s / 3×10¹¹ Pa ≈ 3×10⁸ s, about 10 years of simulated time.

The bounds are also conservative because the measured loss is mostly phonon damping, not flow. Phonon damping lowers Q without any creep happening.

So MD gives two things for the solid:
- the anelastic part, Q and G′ versus frequency for each defect type, which is what you are measuring;
- lower bounds on the flow viscosity, which rise only as the run gets longer.

A real crystal viscosity would need a creep model built from defect mobilities, such as vacancy diffusion (Nabarro–Herring creep) or grain-boundary sliding rates. Those inputs could be extracted from MD, for example the vacancy diffusivity from the Schottky mean-square-displacement file `msd.dat` that is already saved.

### Claude (10-08 09:05)

The Q plot shows G″/G′ versus frequency, the dissipation only. The slope of the modulus is a separate check, and no current figure plots it for the solids. I just ran the check from the sweep data.

**The test.** G′ and G″ are tied together by causality (Kramers–Kronig). When the loss changes only slowly with frequency, the local form holds:

  dG′/d ln ω ≈ (2/π)·G″

In words: a solid that dissipates must stiffen with frequency, at a rate set by its loss. I compared the slope between adjacent sweep frequencies with (2/π) times the mean G″:

| case | band | dG′/d ln ω (GPa) | (2/π)G″ (GPa) | difference / σ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| melt-grown 4000 K shear | 0.2–1 THz | 5.1 ± 0.7 | 5.0 ± 0.5 | 0.1 |
| melt-grown 5000 K shear | 0.2–1 THz | 7.6 ± 1.0 | 7.3 ± 0.6 | 0.3 |
| melt-grown 5000 K shear | 1–5 THz | 11.2 ± 1.7 | 11.9 ± 0.7 | −0.4 |
| melt-grown 5000 K uniaxial | 0.2–1 THz | 9.7 ± 2.0 | 9.8 ± 1.2 | −0.1 |
| perfect B1 shear | 0.2–1 THz | 0.53 ± 0.26 | 0.20 ± 0.21 | 1.0 |
| perfect B1 shear | 1–5 THz | 0.32 ± 0.17 | 0.47 ± 0.17 | −0.6 |
| Schottky shear | 0.2–1 THz | 1.1 ± 1.1 | 8.5 ± 1.3 | **−4.4** |
| bicrystal shear | 1–5 THz | −117 ± 17 | 43 ± 19 | **−6.4** |
| bicrystal uniaxial | 0.2–1 and 1–5 THz | −54, +176 | 6, 26 | **±41** |

**Where it is consistent.** For the melt-grown crystals and perfect B1 between 0.2 and 5 THz, slope and loss agree to within 1σ. This independently confirms that the measured G″ is a genuine linear material response, not noise or thermostat damping.

**Where it fails, and why:**
- **Bicrystal around 1 THz.** This is the slab resonance (f₁ ≈ v_s/L_z). G′ swings negative and then positive across it, which is a property of the cell's geometry, not of the material. The local relation does not apply at a resonance, so the large failure is expected.
- **5 THz points in the uniaxial runs and the 4000 K shear run** (2.5–5σ). These points heat by 50–60 K per run and are already flagged †. The loss there is also changing fast with frequency, which breaks the slowly-varying assumption.
- **Schottky shear at 0.2–1 THz.** G′ is flat while G″ is large, which is not causal for a stationary material. The likely cause is that this cell was still aging during the sweep: its potential energy was drifting, so each frequency measured a slightly different crystal. The aged-Schottky runs now going (job 4233462, at 5, 20 and 50 ps periods) are the direct test.

I can add this as a figure: G′ versus frequency for each solid, with the curve the loss predicts, G′(f₀) + (2/π)∫G″ d ln ω, drawn through it. I'd also put the check in the results table and the methods doc as a standard validation step.

### Claude (10-08 09:05)

Good, that's reassuring. There is one caution before comparing them with seismic or lab values. Ours are measured at 0.02–5 THz, where the loss is mostly phonon damping, plus defect motion in the Schottky and bicrystal cells. Seismic Q (about 100–300 in the lower mantle, at about 1 Hz) comes from dislocation and grain-boundary processes that run about 12 orders of magnitude slower. Similar numbers don't yet mean the same mechanism. To link them, follow how Q changes with frequency in the defect cells as the period gets longer. The aged-Schottky runs and the γ₀ = 0.03 bicrystal runs, at 20 and 50 ps periods, are the first steps in that direction.

Four changes to the figures and docs are still waiting for your go-ahead:
- Replace the solid-phase Green–Kubo circles in fig_vs_T, and draw the pressure line dashed.
- Add `fig_effective_viscosity` to the standard collection run.
- Add a G′-slope versus loss (causality) figure and table.
- Record these points in the methods doc.

All four would apply to the V9.6397 results too. Should I make them?

### Claude (10-08 09:17)

**V9.6397 update:** the 3000 K melt-grown cell is confirmed crystalline (energy dropped 0.55 eV/atom then held steady, 19.7 GPa, strong Bragg peaks). The two follow-on runs are queued:
- the 2000 K Green–Kubo run, starting from this crystal (job 4235938);
- the 3000 K and 2000 K melt-grown sweeps, which submit themselves once each one's equilibrated starting state exists.

Most of the short V9.6397 sweeps have already finished. Early results:
- **Liquid 6000 K:** G″/ω ≈ 3.3–3.7 mPa s from 0.05 to 1 THz. That is about 5 times less viscous than the 6000 K liquid at V6.6868, as expected at lower density.
- **Perfect B1 at 3000 K:** G′ ≈ 120 GPa with almost no loss (Q ≈ 300 at 1 THz). Uniaxial Q is about 17–41.
- **Schottky at 3000 K:** shear Q ≈ 170–260, close to the perfect crystal. Uniaxial loss is similar to the perfect crystal.

**Two results look suspect:**
1. **4000 K liquid at the 20 ps period:** η′ jumps to 69 mPa s, from 7 mPa s at 0.2 THz, with a strong third harmonic (|σ₃/σ₁| = 0.31). That point uses the large γ₀ = 0.32, so it is either nonlinear or the liquid is starting to crystallize at 38 GPa. I'll check its potential energy and structure.
2. **Bicrystal at 3000 K, 5 ps-period shear:** G′ = −3.8 GPa and large harmonics. That is not a linear elastic response. The 1 THz slab resonance is too far away to explain it, so the boundary may be sliding or rebuilding at this density. I'll look at its trajectory.

Your question from before still stands. Should I make the four figure and doc changes (fix fig_vs_T, add the effective-viscosity figure to the collection script, add the G′-slope causality check, record these in the methods doc)?

```
On-the-fly Calculation of Transport Properties of Fluids using the order-n algorithm in Equilibrium Molecular Dynamics. More information on how this plugin works and how this plugin can be used with LAMMPS is thoroughly explained in the work by Jamali et al. J. Chem. Inf. Model. 2019, 59, 4, 1290-1294. If this package is used, please cite the following article:
Jamali SH, Wolff L, Becker TM, de Groen M, Ramdin M, Hartkamp R, Bardow A, Vlugt TJH, Moultos OA. OCTP: A Tool for On-the-Fly Calculation of Transport Properties of Fluids with the Order-n Algorithm in LAMMPS. Journal of Chemical Information and Modeling. 2019, 59, 4, 1290-1294 (DOI: 10.1021/acs.jcim.8b00939).

This plugin consists of two new compute commands (position and rdf/ext) and one new fix command (ordern) for LAMMPS. For more information on the use of these new commands as well as a LAMMPS input file for the calcualation of transport properties for a water-methanol mixture, the user is referred to the work of Jamali et al. 

"compute position" computes a global vector of 5*N components, where N is the total number of atoms in the simulation box. This vector includes the position (x,y,z), the atom ID, and the group mask of each atom. This compute can be used by "fix ordern" to calculate the self-diffusion and Maxwell-Stefan diffusion coefficients of a multicomponent mixture (see J. Chem. Theory Comput., 2018, 14 (5), pp 2667–2677). The components of the mixture should be pre-defined via the command group. The only restriction on the calculation of diffusion coefficients is that no atom should belong to more than 1 group. 

"compute rdf/ext" is an extension of "compute rdf", which is already available in LAMMPS. This compute calculates the RDF up to sqrt(2)/2 of the box length. In addition, RDFs are corrected due to the finite-size of the simulation box are computed. For the finite-size correction, the method of van der Vegt et al. (see J. Chem. Theory Comput., 2013, 9 (3), pp 1347–1355 and J. Phys. Chem. B, 2018, 122 (21), pp 5515–5526) has been implemented. Similar to "compute position", the mixture should be pre-defined via the command group. The restriction on the calculation of RDFs is that no atom should belong to more than 1 group. This compute command directly generates output files; however, in order to invoke it during the simulation, a fix command (e.g., fix ave/time) should be specified.

"fix ordern" uses the modified order-n algorithm by Dubbeldam et al. (Mol. Simul., 2009, 35 (
```

```
# -*- CMake -*- file for plugin examples.
# The is meant to be used as a template for plugins that are
# distributed independent from the LAMMPS package.
##########################################

cmake_minimum_required(VERSION 3.16)

# enforce out-of-source build
if(${CMAKE_SOURCE_DIR} STREQUAL ${CMAKE_BINARY_DIR})
  message(FATAL_ERROR "In-source builds are not allowed. You must create and use a build directory. "
    "Please remove CMakeCache.txt and CMakeFiles first.")
endif()

project(plugins VERSION 1.0 LANGUAGES CXX)

# when this file is included as subdirectory in the LAMMPS build, many settings are directly imported
if(LAMMPS_DIR)
  set(LAMMPS_HEADER_DIR ${LAMMPS_SOURCE_DIR})
else()
  # NOTE: the next line should be commented out when used outside of the LAMMPS package
  get_filename_component(LAMMPS_SOURCE_DIR ${PROJECT_SOURCE_DIR}/../../src ABSOLUTE)
  set(LAMMPS_HEADER_DIR ${LAMMPS_SOURCE_DIR} CACHE PATH "Location of LAMMPS headers")
  if(NOT LAMMPS_HEADER_DIR)
    message(FATAL_ERROR "Must set LAMMPS_HEADER_DIR")
  endif()
  # by default, install into $HOME/.local (not /usr/local),
  # so that no root access (and sudo) is needed
  if(CMAKE_INSTALL_PREFIX_INITIALIZED_TO_DEFAULT)
    set(CMAKE_INSTALL_PREFIX "$ENV{HOME}/.local" CACHE PATH "Default install path" FORCE)
  endif()
  # ugly hacks for MSVC which by default always reports an old C++ standard in the __cplusplus macro
  # and prints lots of pointless warnings about "unsafe" functions
  if(MSVC)
    if(CMAKE_CXX_COMPILER_ID STREQUAL "MSVC")
      add_compile_options(/Zc:__cplusplus)
      add_compile_options(/wd4244)
      add_compile_options(/wd4267)
      add_compile_options(/wd4250)
      add_compile_options(/EHsc)
    endif()
    add_compile_definitions(_CRT_SECURE_NO_WARNINGS)
  endif()
endif()

# we *require* C++11 without extensions but prefer C++17.
# Kokkos requires at least C++17 (currently)
if(NOT CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD)
  if(cxx_std_17 IN_LIST CMAKE_CXX_COMPILE_FEATURES)
    set(CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD 17)
  else()
    set(CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD 11)
  endif()
endif()

if(CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD LESS 17)

endif()
if(PKG_KOKKOS AND (CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD LESS 17))

endif()
# turn off C++17 check in lmptype.h
if(LAMMPS_CXX11)
  add_compile_definitions(LAMMPS_CXX11)
endif()
```

```
################################################################################
# helper function
function(validate_option name values)
  string(TOLOWER ${${name}} needle_lower)
  string(TOUPPER ${${name}} needle_upper)
  list(FIND ${values} ${needle_lower} IDX_LOWER)
  list(FIND ${values} ${needle_upper} IDX_UPPER)
  if(${IDX_LOWER} LESS 0 AND ${IDX_UPPER} LESS 0)
    list_to_bulletpoints(POSSIBLE_VALUE_LIST ${${values}})
    message(FATAL_ERROR "\n########################################################################\n"
```

```
When the style viscosity is chosen, the MSDs of the components of the pressure tensor
are computed. This style accepts the global vector calculated by the command compute
pressure,35 which are the components of the pressure tensor. The output ﬁle consists of 10
columns. The ﬁrst column contains the correlation time, columns 2 to 7 contain the MSD
computed from the three diagonal and the three oﬀ-diagonal components of the pressure ten-
sor, column 8 is the average MSD computed from the three oﬀ-diagonal components and all
six components of the pressure tensor, and the last column contains the MSD corresponding
```

```
# Equilibrium MD for Green-Kubo viscosities of MgO (DeePMD), restartable in chunks.
# Start: read DATA (with velocities), NVT at TEMP for NEQ steps (Nose-Hoover, 0.1 ps), then NVE production.
# Production is NVE so that no thermostat enters the stress correlations (shear or bulk).
#
# Required -var: TEMP (K)  DATA  MODEL  NEQ  NTOT (production steps)  NCHUNK (steps per chunk)
#                NS (sampling interval, steps)  MAXCHUNKS  TIMEOUT (s)
# Outputs: pres.dat    step pxx pyy pzz pxy pxz pyz temp pe etotal  (instantaneous, every NS steps, bar)
#          msd.dat     step msd(Mg) msd(O)  (every 1000 steps; checks that the liquid stays liquid)
#          eq.data     end of NVT equilibration;  restart.gk  after every chunk

units           metal
boundary        p p p
atom_style      atomic
timestep        0.001

variable        HAVE_RS equal is_file(restart.gk)
variable        HAVE_EQ equal is_file(eq.data)
if "${HAVE_RS}" then "read_restart restart.gk" elif "${HAVE_EQ}" "read_data eq.data" else "read_data ${DATA}"
mass            1 24.305
mass            2 16.000
neighbor        1.0 bin
neigh_modify    every 1 check yes
pair_style      deepmd ${MODEL}
pair_coeff      * *
timer           timeout ${TIMEOUT} every 1000

thermo          1000
thermo_style    custom step temp press pe etotal pxy cpuremain

# ---- equilibration (first job only), from the velocities in DATA
if "${HAVE_RS} == 0 && ${HAVE_EQ} == 0" then &
   "velocity all scale ${TEMP}" &
   "fix EQ all nvt temp ${TEMP} ${TEMP} 0.1" &
   "run ${NEQ}" &
   "unfix EQ" &
   "reset_timestep 0" &
   "write_data eq.data"

# ---- NVE production
fix             NVE all nve
group           Mg type 1
group           O  type 2
compute         msdMg Mg msd com yes
compute         msdO  O  msd com yes
variable        pxx equal pxx
variable        pyy equal pyy
variable        pzz equal pzz
variable        pxy equal pxy
variable        pxz equal pxz
variable        pyz equal pyz
variable        tt  equal temp
variable        pe  equal pe
variable        et  equal etotal
variable        mMg equal c_msdMg[4]
variable        mO  equal c_msdO[4]
fix             PAV all ave/time ${NS} 1 ${NS} v_pxx v_pyy v_pzz v_pxy v_pxz v_pyz v_tt v_pe v_et append pres.dat
fix             MAV all ave/time 1000 1 1000 v_mMg v_mO append msd.dat

variable        NXT equal ternary((floor(step/${NCHUNK})+1)*${NCHUNK}<${NTOT},(floor(step/${NCHUNK})+1)*${NCHUNK},${NTOT})
label           loop
variable        ic loop ${MAXCHUNKS}
  if "$(
```

### Claude (10-08 09:38)

I've installed OCTP as a separate add-on and started a test run. Your production LAMMPS wasn't rebuilt or modified, so the running jobs aren't affected. There is one important point first, because it affects what OCTP can do for you.

**OCTP does not take multiple time steps.** I read both papers and the code. The "order-n algorithm" is a way of recording data, not of integrating faster:
- The MD runs at the same 1 fs step and the same cost.
- OCTP keeps the time integral of the stress in a ladder of blocks spaced 1, 10, 100, … steps apart, with 10 blocks of 10 elements each by default. This tracks correlations from 1 step out to 10¹⁰ steps without storing the stress history.
- It then gets η from the Einstein form, η = d/dt [V/(2k_BT)·⟨(∫P dt)²⟩], whose slope is the same quantity as the Green–Kubo running integral.

So it won't reach lower frequencies than we already do. The lowest usable frequency is still set by how long the run is and by the noise, and our Green–Kubo runs already save the stress every 2 steps for 1.5 ns, so any correlation time can be computed offline. What OCTP may offer is a smoother, less noisy long-time estimate, because the Einstein form integrates out some noise. The test is meant to check exactly that.

**How it's installed (CMake):**
- I built it as a LAMMPS runtime plugin, `build_octp_plugin/octpplugin.so`, from your clone at `src/OCTP`. Your LAMMPS was built with the PLUGIN package, so an input file just adds `plugin load …/octpplugin.so`.
- It compiled cleanly against 22Jul2025 with the same conda compiler and MPI.
- I didn't touch `liblammps.so` or `lmp`. The alternative was rebuilding LAMMPS with OCTP compiled in and reinstalling it into the conda environment, which is the version every running job uses.
- The wrapper source (`octpplugin.cpp` and `CMakeLists.txt`) is in `tools/octp_plugin/`. I didn't add files to your git clone.

**The test** (job 4236292, about 1.5 h on a P100):
- 8000 K liquid at V6.6868, starting from the equilibrated Green–Kubo cell: 5 ps NVT, then 120 ps NVE.
- `fix ordern viscosity` runs alongside every-step stress output, so OCTP and Green–Kubo see the same trajectory.
- `tools/octp_compare.py` will plot OCTP's η(t) (both the slope and MSD/t) against the Green–Kubo running integral, and I'll check OCTP's speed overhead.

The reference value is η₀ = 8.2 mPa s. I'll report when it finishes.

**Tool `Bash`** — Add KK table, KK figure, effective-viscosity call

```
cd /resnick/home/recohen/Claude/tools; python - <<'EOF'
p='anelastic_collect.py'; s=open(p).read()
# 1) helper + figure, placed before main
helper = '''def kk_pairs(s):
    """Local Kramers-Kronig check between adjacent sweep frequencies (Eq. 15):
    dM'/dln w (finite difference) against (2/pi) <M''> (mean of the two points). Returns rows of
    (f1, f2, slope, dslope, kk, dkk, z = (slope - kk)/sigma)."""
    R = sorted(s['rows'], key=lambda r: r['f'])
    out = []
    for a, b in zip(R[:-1], R[1:]):
        L = np.log(b['f'] / a['f'])
        sl, dsl = (b['Mp'] - a['Mp']) / L, np.hypot(a['dMp'], b['dMp']) / L
        kk, dkk = (a['Mpp'] + b['Mpp']) / np.pi, np.hypot(a['dMpp'], b['dMpp']) / np.pi
        out.append((a['f'], b['f'], sl, dsl, kk, dkk, (sl - kk) / np.hypot(dsl, dkk)))
    return out

def fig_kk(S, out):
    """Storage modulus vs frequency for the crystal sweeps, with the curve predicted from the loss by Eq. 15,
    integrated upward from the lowest-frequency point."""
    sol = [s for s in S if s['valid'] and s['T'] not in LIQ and len(s['rows']) > 1]
    if not sol:
```

<details><summary>result of Bash</summary>

```
### 1.6 Finite-size resonance (bicrystal, any slab)

When the drive approaches the lowest transverse standing wave across the cell,

$$f_1\approx\frac{v_s}{L_z} \tag{13}$$

the response becomes inertial rather than anelastic. Signs are large harmonics, non-monotonic $G'$ and $M'$, and huge error bars. For MgO at 59.6 Å, $f_1$ ≈ 1 THz. Only $f\ll f_1$ is quasi-static. Compliant interfaces lower $f_1$ further.

## 2. Protocol that worked

1. **Model.** Compress the DeePMD model with `dp --tf compress -i graph.pb -o graph_c.pb` (deepmd_py311 env), which runs 2.8× faster on a P100. Verify with `tools/compare_dp_models.py A B data...`: forces, energy and virial on several configurations. MgO gave ΔF = 1 µeV/Å rms.
2. **Cell.** Use 4096 atoms, a cube at fixed V. This fits an 8×8×8 rock-salt crystal exactly, with $a=L/8$.
3. **Liquid temperatures.** For each liquid T:
   - Start GK from the previous T's equilibrated state (`GK/eq.data`) or a known liquid, with 20 ps NVT at T then 1.5 ns NVE.
   - Run the shear sweep (P = 0.2, 1, 5, 20, 100 ps) and the uniaxial sweep (Table §1.5).
   - Both sweeps copy `GK/eq.data` into each task directory, so there is no re-equilibration.
4. **Cooling.** Step down at constant V until the liquid crystallizes. The signs are a sudden PE drop (MgO: −0.46 eV/atom), a pressure drop (−23 GPa at V6.6868) and an MSD plateau.
   - In NVE the latent heat raises T by thousands of K: 6000 K → 8490 K at 1.05 ns. Cut the analysis before the event (`GKCUT`).
   - The supercooled liquid can survive for > 1 ns and then crystallize, so always check PE across the whole NVE run.
5. **Solid at two temperatures.**
   - **Melt-grown crystal:** GK plus the reduced 6-task sweep (shear and uniaxial at 0.2, 1 and 5 THz; γ₀ = ε₀ = 0.01; 200 ps each). It comes out rotated or polycrystalline relative to the box, so check it with `tools/xtal_check.py`.
   - **Controlled-defect references at the same T** (`tools/build_defect_crystals.py`):
     - Perfect, box-aligned B1 (`schottky --npair 0`).
     - 20 bound Schottky pairs (Zener-type relaxation).
     - Σ5 (310)/[001] symmetric tilt bicrystal, boundary normal z (`--normal-z`), so the xz shear slides the boundaries.
   - Each defect cell gets 50 ps NVT, 1.5 ns NVE GK, the 6-task sweep, and low-frequency shear at P = 20 and 50 ps:
     - Schottky: γ₀ = 0.02, 0.8–1 M steps.
     - Bicrystal: γ₀ = 0.03, 1–1.25 M steps.
   - Schottky cells age: vacancies cluster and anneal, at 5000 K PE drops 28 eV within 150 ps. R
… [5915 more characters]
```

```
cd /resnick/home/recohen/Claude/Anelasticity; python - <<'EOF'
p='ANELASTICITY_METHODS.md'; s=open(p).read()
sec17 = r'''
### 1.7 Crystal: what Green–Kubo measures, effective viscosity, and a causality check

A crystal does not flow on MD time scales. Its full stress relaxation function levels off at the static modulus, $G(t\to\infty)=G_{eq}>0$, so $\int G\,dt$ diverges. Green–Kubo at fixed cell shape sees only the stress fluctuations: it drops the Born (static) term. The fluctuation $G(t)$ then decays on the phonon relaxation time τ (Akhiezer-type damping). Its integral is **internal friction, not a viscosity**. Two consequences:

- **Opposite T slopes.** For a single relaxation, $G''/\omega=\Delta G\,\tau/(1+\omega^2\tau^2)$. GK gives the $\omega\tau\ll1$ side, $\Delta G\,\tau$, which falls with T. The sweeps at 0.2–5 THz have $\omega\tau\gtrsim1$, giving $\Delta G/(\omega^2\tau)$, which rises with T. At the same frequency the two agree: MgO 4000 K at 1 THz gives GK 1.73 mPa s against the sweep's 1.76 ± 0.18.
- **Shear channels in an aligned crystal.** The GK average over five components mixes C₄₄-type (off-diagonal) and (C₁₁−C₁₂)/2-type (normal-difference) shear. An xz sweep on 
… [3169 more characters]
```

Green–Kubo at fixed cell shape sees only the stress fluctuations: it drops the Born (static) term. The fluctuation $G(t)$ then decays on the phonon relaxation time τ (Akhiezer-type damping). Its integral is **internal friction, not a viscosity**. Two consequences:
```
127:$$\eta_0\ \ge\ \frac{G'\,Q}{\omega},\qquad Q=\frac{G'}{G''+2\sigma_{G''}} \tag{14}$$
```

**Tool `Bash`** — Add OCTP/respa note to methods doc §4

```
cd /resnick/home/recohen/Claude/Anelasticity; python - <<'EOF'
p='ANELASTICITY_METHODS.md'; s=open(p).read()
add = '''
**OCTP and longer times.**
- **What OCTP is.** OCTP's `fix ordern` is order-n (multiple-window) *sampling* of the Einstein form, $\\eta=\\lim_t \\frac{d}{dt}\\frac{V}{2k_BT}\\langle(\\int_0^tP_{\\alpha\\beta}dt')^2\\rangle$. It is not a multiple-time-step integrator, so MD cost and the lowest reachable frequency are unchanged. The Einstein slope equals the GK running integral. `pres.dat` already holds the stress every 2 fs, so any lag can be computed offline.
- **r-RESPA is not available.** `pair deepmd` sets `respa_enable = 0`: the network is one non-separable force with no inner/outer split.
- **Paths to longer times:**
  - a larger time step after an energy-drift test (≤ 2×);
  - several GPUs per run;
  - independent replicas for noise;
  - a cheaper surrogate potential, fitted to the DeePMD model, for long runs;
  - for defect relaxations, rates from barriers (NEB/kMC) with relaxation strengths from MD.
'''
anchor = '\n## 5. Trajectories for animations'
assert anchor in s
s = s.replace(anchor, add + anchor, 1)
open(p,'w').write(s)
EOF
echo ok
```

### Claude (10-08 09:51)

The four figure and doc changes are done, and I've sent you the updated `fig_vs_T` and the new `fig_kk`. `fig_effective_viscosity` didn't arrive because the upload server timed out (504); it is the same as the version I sent earlier, and I can resend it in a few minutes.

**The four changes:**
1. **`fig_vs_T`:** in panel (a) the crystal Green–Kubo circles are gone. In their place are upward arrows for the lower bound on the crystal's flow viscosity, η₀ ≥ G′Q/ω, for the melt-grown crystal and each defect cell. The pressure line is now dashed and labelled "right axis".
2. **Effective viscosity:** the collection script now produces `fig_effective_viscosity` on every run, for any volume or material.
3. **Causality check:** a new figure, `fig_kk`, plots G′ versus frequency with the curve predicted from the loss. A new results table (§2a) compares the slope dG′/d ln ω with (2/π)G″. The melt-grown and perfect crystals agree within 1σ. The failures are the expected ones: the bicrystal slab resonance, the heated 5 THz points, and the unaged Schottky cell.
4. **Methods doc:** a new §1.7 explains:
   - why crystal Green–Kubo is internal friction, not viscosity;
   - why the slopes versus T look opposite;
   - the two shear channels in an aligned crystal;
   - the flow-viscosity bound (Eq. 14) and the causality check (Eq. 15).

   I also added both points to the pitfalls list, plus a note on OCTP and longer run times.

**r-RESPA: not with this model.** The DeePMD pair style explicitly turns r-RESPA off (`respa_enable = 0`). r-RESPA needs forces that split into fast and slow parts, like bonds versus long-range forces, and the neural-network force is a single block. It would also gain little here: the expensive step is the network evaluation, which r-RESPA would still have to do every outer step. OCTP doesn't help with speed either, because it changes how data is recorded, not how fast the simulation runs.

**Realistic ways to run longer:**
- **Larger time step:** up to about 2×, if a short test shows acceptable energy drift. Oxygen vibrations limit how far it can go.
- **Several GPUs per run:** perhaps 2–3× for 4096 atoms.
- **Independent replicas:** reduce noise, but don't reach lower frequencies.
- **A fast surrogate potential:** a classical or small machine-learned potential fitted to the DeePMD model could be 100–1000× faster. That is the only route to the 10–1000× longer runs you're hoping for, at some cost in accuracy, so it would need checking against DeePMD on a few cases.
- **Rates instead of trajectories, for the defect cells:** take the activation barriers from NEB or kinetic Monte Carlo and the relaxation strengths from these runs. That puts the defect loss peak at any frequency, including seismic ones.

The OCTP comparison against Green–Kubo will run automatically when job 4236292 finishes, in about 75 minutes.

### Claude (10-08 10:05)

You're right to doubt it. A single activation energy hides several assumptions, and our runs can test some of them directly. On kinetic Monte Carlo (kMC): yes, with some additions, but not in the current LAMMPS build as it stands.

**What's questionable in a single-activation-energy description.** Karato writes Q⁻¹ ∝ (ω·τ₀·e^(E*/RT))^(−α). That bundles four assumptions:
- **One activated mechanism with a fixed E*.** Our crystal results already show at least two loss sources:
  - phonon damping, which is not thermally activated and is what we see at THz;
  - defect motion, which is activated.

  A spread of barriers widens the loss band, so the fitted α and E* then depend on which frequency and temperature window you look at.
- **A temperature-independent relaxation strength.** For intrinsic defects the defect concentration goes as e^(−H_f/kT), so an apparent E* mixes the formation energy with the migration barrier. Zener-type strengths also scale as 1/T.
- **Time–temperature superposition:** Q⁻¹ depends only on ωτ(T). This is testable. If the Q⁻¹(ω, T) curves for one cell at several temperatures don't collapse onto a single curve when frequency is rescaled by e^(E/kT), a single E* fails.
- **Transition-state behaviour near melting.** At 5000 K, kT = 0.43 eV, so barriers are only a few kT. Motion becomes collective, and Arrhenius extrapolation over 12 decades in frequency becomes doubtful.

**A symmetry argument against simple pair reorientation.** A nearest-neighbour Mg–O vacancy pair points along ⟨100⟩. In a cubic crystal, such a defect can only relax the (C₁₁−C₁₂)/2 shear, not C₄₄ (Nowick and Berry). But the Schottky cell shows strong C₄₄ (xz) loss: Q ≈ 10, against more than 155 for the perfect crystal. So the dominant loss there is something else, probably vacancy hops or cluster motion. That needs to be identified before any rate model is built.

**kMC with our setup:**
- **Barriers (nudged elastic band, NEB):** our production LAMMPS doesn't include the REPLICA package, which provides NEB, parallel replica dynamics, temperature-accelerated dynamics and most of hyperdynamics. I checked the library's symbols. Two ways around it:
  1. Use ASE's NEB and dimer methods with the DeePMD Python calculator. Both are installed in the `deepmd_py311` environment. This is easy for a cell of about 500–1000 atoms on one GPU, and it is what I'd use.
  2. Build REPLICA as a second LAMMPS or a plugin, as I did for OCTP, which also leaves the production build untouched.
- **The kMC itself:** in a crystal the lattice is fixed, so a small on-lattice kMC with Mg and O vacancies on the rock-salt sublattices is enough. I'd write it in Python.
  - **Rate catalog:** hop barriers that depend on the local environment, from NEB.
  - **Strain coupling:** each barrier shifts with the applied strain through the elastic dipole tensors of the saddle and initial states.
  - **Output:** driving kMC with an oscillating strain gives Q⁻¹(ω, T) directly at any frequency, down to seismic.
- **Off-lattice alternative:** off-lattice kMC codes that find events on the fly, such as EON or k-ART, can call LAMMPS or ASE for forces. They are heavier to set up and only needed if the events don't fit a lattice, for example grain boundaries.

**Proposed order** (I added this to the TODO list as T17):
1. **Find the events, cheaply.** Run short MD of the Schottky cell at 3–4 temperatures at fixed V, saving positions often. Locate vacancies with a Wigner–Seitz analysis and count each event type, which also tests whether each rate is Arrhenius. About 1–2 P100-hours per temperature.
2. **NEB with ASE and DeePMD** for the events that matter, at the same volume, with harmonic transition-state-theory prefactors to compare against the MD rates.
3. **Elastic dipole tensors** from relaxed defect cells. These give the relaxation strengths and which shear channel each mechanism couples to.
4. **On-lattice kMC under oscillating strain** to get Q⁻¹(ω, T). Validate it against our direct MD sweeps at the high temperatures where they overlap, then extrapolate.

Should I start step 1? It reuses the existing scripts with a trajectory dump. I'd use the V6.6868 Schottky cell at 3500, 4000, 4500 and 5000 K, about 6 P100-hours in all.

## User (10-08 10:15)

Which is better ase or replica?

### Claude (10-08 10:15)

I'd use ASE for this project and add REPLICA later only if we need what it adds beyond NEB.

| | ASE + DeePMD Python | LAMMPS REPLICA (`neb`) |
|---|---|---|
| Available now | Yes, already in `deepmd_py311` with the same `graph_c.pb` | No. It needs a second build or a plugin, like OCTP |
| Saddle-point methods | NEB, climbing-image NEB, AutoNEB, dimer (finds saddles without knowing the final state) | NEB only |
| Attempt frequencies (prefactors) | Built-in vibrational analysis gives them directly | Needs a separate frequency calculation |
| Elastic dipole tensors | Stress per configuration, scripted in a loop | Possible, but through input-file plumbing |
| Feeding the kMC | Same language: barriers, dipoles and rates go straight into the Python kMC | Needs a file per event and log parsing |
| Speed | Images computed one after another on one GPU; fine for 500–1000-atom cells (minutes per path) | Faster for large cells, with parallel images |
| Extras | — | Parallel replica dynamics, temperature-accelerated dynamics and hyperdynamics (accelerated MD), which ASE lacks |

ASE is better for barriers, prefactors and dipoles, because:
- small point-defect cells are enough;
- the dimer method can find events we haven't guessed;
- everything flows directly into the kMC.

REPLICA is worth building only if:
- we need saddle searches in large grain-boundary cells (thousands of atoms);
- we want accelerated MD. Its gain would be small at our temperatures, since the barriers are only a few kT, but it could matter at mantle temperatures.

One check first: the ASE DeePMD calculator must give the same energy, forces and stress as LAMMPS for the same configuration, because stress sign and unit conventions differ between the two. That's a quick test on a GPU node with `compare_dp_models.py`, adapted for ASE.

### Claude (10-08 10:19)

Step 1 has started, and ASE is set up and checked against LAMMPS.

**ASE check passed** (job 4238086). On the 5000 K Schottky cell (4056 atoms, on the GPU), ASE with the DeePMD calculator reproduces LAMMPS:
- energy to within 10⁻¹¹ eV;
- forces to within 5×10⁻⁹ eV/Å;
- stress to within 0.1 bar out of 1.4 Mbar.

ASE is safe to use for the barrier (NEB), dimer, attempt-frequency and elastic-dipole calculations. The check script is `tools/ase_dp_check.py`. One gotcha: batch jobs must call `$CONDA_PREFIX/bin/python`, because plain `python` picks up a different interpreter without DeePMD.

**Hop-statistics runs** (job 4238025, 4 tasks, in `V6.6868/4096atom/DefectHops/<T>K`):
- **Start:** the fresh 20-pair Schottky cell (`B1_schottky20.data`) at 3500, 4000, 4500 and 5000 K, at fixed V6.6868.
- **Run:** 10 ps NVT equilibration, then 200 ps NVT with positions saved every 0.1 ps.
- **Cost:** about 2.5 P100-hours each, about 10 in total. That's more than the 6 I estimated, because I lengthened the runs to get enough hops at 3500 K.

**Analysis tool** (`tools/vacancy_hops.py`):
- It averages positions over 0.5 ps to remove thermal vibration, then assigns each atom to the nearest Mg or O lattice site.
- Empty sites are vacancies; an atom changing site is a hop, classified by jump vector.
- It reports, at each temperature, the vacancy counts, the hop types, and the hop rate per vacancy for Mg and O.
- It also fits an apparent activation energy and attempt prefactor across the four temperatures. That is the first test of whether a single activation energy holds.

I'll test the analysis on the first 30 ps of the 5000 K run as soon as it's written, in about 25 minutes.

### Claude (10-08 10:45)

The analysis is validated on the first 30 ps at 5000 K (148.7 GPa):
- **Vacancies:** the cell keeps 20 Mg and 20 O vacancies. A few extra appear briefly while atoms are mid-hop.
- **Hop types:** every hop is to a nearest-neighbour site on the same sublattice, a distance of a/√2.
- **Rates:** counting jumps directly is unreliable, because atoms often hop and immediately hop back as they vibrate. The count therefore depends on how long an atom must stay on its new site to count, falling from 0.43 to 0.24 per ps for Mg as that minimum time goes from 0 to 2 ps.
- **Fix:** I now measure the rate from on-lattice tracer diffusion, where a hop and its return cancel. The jump rate per vacancy is Γ = 6D*/(f·c_v·λ²), with f = 0.78, c_v the vacancy fraction, and λ = a/√2 the jump length. At 5000 K:

  | | D* (Å²/ps) | Γ per vacancy (/ps) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Mg | 2.6×10⁻³ | 0.27 |
  | O | 1.6×10⁻³ | 0.17 |

  So each vacancy jumps about once every 4–6 ps, with O slower than Mg. The counted rates are still printed alongside as a cross-check.

The four 200 ps runs finish in about 2 hours. The analysis then runs automatically across 3500–5000 K and gives the first test of whether a single activation energy describes these rates. The OCTP comparison will also arrive when its job ends.

### Claude (10-08 11:05)

The OCTP test works, but OCTP gives us nothing our Green–Kubo analysis doesn't already provide. I've sent you the comparison figure (`results/fig_octp_vs_gk`). The test was the 8000 K liquid, 120 ps NVE, with OCTP and the every-step stress output recorded from the same trajectory.

- **Correctness:** OCTP's Einstein slope matches the Green–Kubo running integral from 2 fs to 2 ps. Its plateau, about 8.2 mPa s between 0.2 and 2 ps, matches η₀ = 8.19 mPa s from our 1.5 ns Green–Kubo run. The plugin build and the unit conversion are both right.
- **Cost:** 23.8 steps/s, the same as without OCTP, so the overhead is zero.
- **No gain at long times:** beyond about 3 ps both curves are dominated by noise in a 120 ps run, and OCTP is no less noisy. That's expected, since the two methods are mathematically equivalent. Its other estimate, MSD/t, converges more slowly and reads low at short times.

OCTP's only practical benefit is that it doesn't need the stress series saved to disk. Longer or lower-frequency results still need more simulated time or the rate-based (kMC) approach. I've added this to `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` §5.

The hop-statistics runs at 3500–5000 K finish in about 1.5 hours, and the Arrhenius analysis of the vacancy jump rates then runs automatically.

### Claude (10-08 12:36)

Step 1 is done, and the result supports your doubt about a single activation energy. I've sent you the Arrhenius plot (`results/fig_vacancy_arrhenius`).

**Vacancy jump rates** in the Schottky cell at V6.6868 (138–148 GPa, 200 ps at each temperature):

| T (K) | Mg rate (ps⁻¹) | O rate (ps⁻¹) |
|---|---|---|
| 3500 | 0.020 | 0.0093 |
| 4000 | 0.089 | 0.062 |
| 4500 | 0.195 | 0.156 |
| 5000 | 0.337 | 0.265 |

- **Mechanism:** more than 95 % of jumps go to the nearest site on the same sublattice. Second-neighbour jumps rise to about 4 % of all jumps at 5000 K. Oxygen is slower than Mg at every temperature.
- **The apparent activation energy falls with temperature:**

  | interval (K) | Mg (eV) | O (eV) |
  |---|---|---|
  | 3500–4000 | 3.6 | 4.6 |
  | 4000–4500 | 2.4 | 2.8 |
  | 4500–5000 | 2.1 | 2.1 |

  The 3500 K rates are uncertain by about ±13 % (about 55 O jumps), so this curvature is real. A single fit gives 2.84 eV for Mg and 3.38 eV for O, and the 3500 K points fall well below it.
- **What this means for extrapolation:** an activation energy fitted at high temperature already overestimates the oxygen rate by about 3× at 3500 K, and the error grows further down.

**Possible causes,** which the MD alone can't separate:
- extra defects appear thermally above 4500 K (vacancies rise from 20 to as many as 36);
- vacancies cluster and bind (the potential energy drifts down during the runs);
- the barrier itself may soften through anharmonic and entropic effects close to melting.

**Step 2 would separate them.** I'd compute static NEB barriers with ASE and DeePMD at the same volume for:
- isolated Mg and O vacancy jumps;
- the jump of a vacancy in a bound Mg–O pair;
- the second-neighbour jump.

With harmonic transition-state prefactors from ASE's vibrational analysis, this would show whether the 3.6–4.6 eV low-temperature values are the true barriers and what lowers them near melting. It needs a 512–1000-atom cell and runs of minutes per pathway on a P100, about 1–2 GPU-hours in total. Should I go ahead?

I've added the hop results as §5a of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` and updated item T17 in the TODO list.

### Claude (10-08 13:10)

I've started step 2 as job 4246743, which runs `tools/neb_vacancy.py` (ASE with DeePMD) at fixed volume V6.6868, so a = 3.7679 Å.

**What it calculates**, all with climbing-image NEB on 7 intermediate images:

| event | jump |
|---|---|
| isolated Mg vacancy | nearest-neighbour jump (a/√2) |
| isolated O vacancy | nearest-neighbour jump |
| bound Mg–O vacancy pair, Mg jump that keeps the pair bound | rotates the pair axis 90°: the Zener reorientation step |
| bound pair, Mg jump that separates the pair | — |
| bound pair, O jump that keeps the pair bound | — |
| bound pair, O jump that separates the pair | — |

It also calculates the pair binding energy and a size check, repeating the isolated Mg barrier in a 512-atom cell against the 1000-atom cell used for everything else.

**For each event it reports:**
- the static barrier;
- the harmonic attempt frequency (Vineyard, from a local Hessian of atoms within 5 Å of the jump);
- how the barrier changes with each strain component, from the stress difference between the saddle point and the starting state. That is the elastic-dipole input for step 3 and for strain-driven kMC.

**What it will tell us:**
- Comparing these barriers with the MD apparent energies (3.6–4.6 eV at 3500–4000 K, about 2.1 eV at 5000 K) shows whether the curvature comes from the barrier itself or from clustering, binding and thermally created defects.
- The pair reorientation and separation barriers show whether a bound pair can reorient without breaking up.

**Caveat:** DeePMD has no explicit charges, so an isolated vacancy here is simply a missing atom in a cell that is no longer stoichiometric. The bound pair is stoichiometric and is the cleaner case.

The 512-atom size check should report within about 15–30 minutes, and the whole set should take a few hours.

### Claude (10-08 13:12)

The first barrier is in, from the 512-atom cell (static pressure 116 GPa at this volume):
- **Isolated Mg-vacancy jump:** barrier 3.77 eV, attempt frequency 23 THz, and a single clean saddle in the middle of the path. It took under a minute, so the full set will be quick.
- **Comparison with MD:** the static barrier matches the MD apparent activation energy at 3500–4000 K (3.6 eV). So the low-temperature MD rates follow ordinary transition-state behaviour. The drop toward about 2.1 eV at 4500–5000 K is not the barrier itself; it must come from something else: thermally created defects, clustering, or anharmonic softening.
- **Strain sensitivity:** the barrier for a jump in the xy plane shifts by about ±15 eV per unit strain, including the xy shear component. A 1 % strain therefore moves it by about 0.15 eV, or 0.35 kT at 5000 K, which is a strong coupling.

The 1000-atom run of all six events, plus the pair binding energy, is in progress. I'll report the full table when it finishes.

### Claude (10-08 13:28)

Step 2 is done. In the 1000-atom cell at V6.6868 (static pressure 116 GPa), the barriers are converged: the Mg value differs by only 0.002 eV between 512 and 1000 atoms.

| jump | barrier (eV) | attempt frequency (THz) |
|---|---|---|
| isolated Mg vacancy | 3.77 | 24 |
| isolated O vacancy | 4.65 | 22 |
| **bound pair, Mg jump that rotates the pair** | **2.57** | 40 |
| **bound pair, O jump that rotates the pair** | **3.09** | 46 |
| bound pair, Mg jump that separates it | 4.45 (end state +2.16 eV) | 27 |
| bound pair, O jump that separates it | 5.11 | 38 |

The Mg–O vacancy pair is strongly bound: separating it costs 3.25 eV.

**Comparison with the MD rates** (per vacancy, ps⁻¹), using harmonic transition-state theory (TST) with no fitted parameters:

| T (K) | Mg, pair rotation (TST) | Mg, MD | O, pair rotation (TST) | O, MD |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3500 | 0.032 | 0.034 | 0.0066 | 0.014 |
| 4000 | 0.092 | 0.14 | 0.024 | 0.079 |
| 4500 | 0.21 | 0.28 | 0.065 | 0.23 |
| 5000 | 0.41 | 0.47 | 0.14 | 0.38 |

- **Mg:** almost all Mg vacancy motion in the MD is rotation around the bound O vacancy. The static 2.57 eV barrier reproduces the MD rate within 1.0–1.5× from 3500 to 5000 K. Free-vacancy migration is 10–30× slower and plays little part.
- **O:** it moves 2–3× faster than pair rotation predicts, so another oxygen mechanism is operating, possibly cluster moves or the extra defects that appear above 4500 K.

**On Karato's single activation energy:** each elementary barrier is well defined and doesn't change with temperature. What changes is the mix of mechanisms (pair rotation, dissociation, free migration, thermally created defects), so a single fitted activation energy is a temperature-dependent average. My earlier diffusion-based estimate also assumed uncorrelated free vacancies, while pair rotation is strongly correlated, which exaggerated the apparent curvature.

**What this means for anelasticity** (preliminary, until steps 3 and 4):
- **Which shear it affects:** the bound pair points along ⟨100⟩, so by symmetry its rotation (a Zener relaxation) affects only the (C₁₁−C₁₂)/2 shear, not C₄₄.
- **Consistent with the sweeps:** at 5000 K the predicted relaxation time is about 1.2 ps, a loss peak near 0.13 THz. That fits the extra uniaxial loss of the Schottky cell at 0.2 THz (Q 42, against 71 for the perfect crystal).
- **The C₄₄ loss has another source:** the large xz-shear loss in the Schottky cell must come from other defects, likely clusters formed as the cell aged.
- **Seismic frequencies:** with these barriers, the pair-rotation peak reaches 1 Hz only near 950 K at this density. At mantle temperatures it sits at MHz–GHz, so bound Schottky pairs by themselves would give almost no seismic attenuation. This still needs the relaxation strength from step 3 and the kMC of step 4.

The full write-up is §5b of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md`, and TODO item T17 is updated.

**Next, step 3:** compute the pair's elastic dipole, which gives its relaxation strength and confirms which shear it affects. It reuses the same ASE setup and takes a few minutes of GPU time. I can also look for the extra oxygen mechanism in the 5000 K trajectories, and run the same barriers at V9.6397 for the pressure dependence. Should I go ahead with any of these?

```
## 5c. Elastic dipoles and Zener relaxation strengths (T17 step 3)

Setup:

- **Dipole tensor:** $G_{ij}=V\,[s_{ij}({\rm defect})-s_{ij}({\rm perfect})]$, so that $E(\varepsilon)=E(0)+G:\varepsilon$.
- **Relaxation strength:** the relaxed reduction of the modulus $M_E=E{:}C{:}E$ for a strain pattern $E$ is

$$\Delta M_E=\frac{n_d}{k_BT}\,{\rm Var}_\alpha\!\left(G^\alpha:E\right) \tag{11}$$

  over the cubic orientation variants α. The Debye peak height is $Q^{-1}_{max}=\Delta M_E/(2M_E)$.
- **Static elastic constants** at this V (116 GPa): C₁₁ = 1287, C₁₂ = 267, C₄₄ = 218, C′ = 510 GPa.

| pair, 2NN ⟨111⟩ | +1.73 | 17.89 diagonal, 0.46 off-diagonal | C₄₄ only | ΔC₄₄ = 0.06 GPa (0.03 %) |
```

### Claude (10-08 13:55)

Step 3 is done, and it changes the picture: rotation of bound vacancy pairs (Zener relaxation) is negligible. The run used the 1000-atom cell at V6.6868; the static elastic constants there are C₁₁ = 1287, C₁₂ = 267, C₄₄ = 218 and C′ = 510 GPa, at 116 GPa static pressure.

**Elastic dipoles and Zener relaxation strengths** at 5000 K, for the MD cell's concentration (20 pairs per 4096 atoms):

| configuration | energy above bound pair | which modulus it relaxes | relaxation |
|---|---|---|---|
| bound pair, nearest-neighbour ⟨100⟩ | 0 | C′ and C₁₁ only | ΔC₁₁ = 0.12 GPa (0.01 %) |
| pair, second-neighbour ⟨111⟩ | +1.73 eV | C₄₄ only | ΔC₄₄ = 0.06 GPa |
| pair, third-neighbour ⟨210⟩ | +2.16 eV | all three | ΔC₁₁ = 1.65 GPa, if every pair were in this state |
| isolated vacancies | — | none (cubic symmetry) | 0 |

- **Selection rules confirmed:** a ⟨100⟩ pair relaxes only C′ and C₁₁, and a ⟨111⟩ pair only C₄₄.
- **The bound pair is nearly isotropic:** its dipole is about 21 eV on each axis, with an anisotropy of only 1.4 eV. Its Zener loss peak is therefore only Q⁻¹ ≈ 5×10⁻⁵, wherever in frequency it sits.

**So Zener relaxation doesn't explain the measured defect loss.** The Schottky cell's extra uniaxial loss at 0.2 THz needs a relaxation of about 20 GPa, roughly 100× more than any vacancy orientation can provide. The THz defect loss must be dynamic instead: either phonons scattering off the vacancies, or the transient stress of atoms caught partway through a jump. The barriers are very sensitive to strain (about 15 eV per unit strain), and a rough estimate of the jump-transient mechanism gives a few GPa, the right order of magnitude. Neither mechanism shifts to seismic frequencies the way a Zener peak would.

**Two other results:**
- **Thermal defects:** forming a bound Schottky pair costs 2.44 eV at fixed volume (5.69 eV for a separated pair). Thermal pairs should appear near 5000 K, which explains the vacancy count rising above 4500 K in the MD.
- **Implication for seismic attenuation:** in this model, vacancies and vacancy pairs give negligible Zener loss at any frequency. Seismic-band defect attenuation would have to come from dislocations, grain boundaries, or charged and aliovalent defects such as Fe³⁺ or H⁺, which DeePMD doesn't represent.

I've added this as §5c of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` (Eq. 11) and updated T17.

**What this means for step 4:** a vacancy kMC would now mainly confirm a negligible result. I'd suggest two things instead:
1. Test the dynamic-loss mechanism cheaply. If the Schottky cell's extra loss scales with the jump rate (Arrhenius in T), it comes from jump transients; if it follows T almost linearly, it comes from phonon scattering. This reuses the sweep setup at 2–3 temperatures.
2. Point the rates approach at grain boundaries, using the bicrystal: boundary sliding barriers and their strain coupling. That is where a real low-frequency, thermally activated loss peak is expected, and where kMC or rate theory would be worth building.

Which would you like next?

### Claude (10-08 17:09)

Two of the long low-frequency runs at V6.6868 have finished, and one gives the first clear grain-boundary signal.

**The bicrystal boundaries slide at 3 % strain.** These are the γ₀ = 0.03 runs at 20 and 50 GHz, 24–48 cycles each:

| f | γ₀ | G′ (GPa) | G″ (GPa) | Q | η′ (Pa s) | third harmonic |σ₃/σ₁| |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 50 GHz | 0.01 | 129 ± 8 | 6 ± 8 | > 6 | — | small |
| 50 GHz | 0.03 | 32 ± 2 | 53 ± 2 | 0.62 | 0.17 | 0.21 |
| 20 GHz | 0.01 | 108 ± 15 | 15 ± 12 | > 2.7 | — | small |
| 20 GHz | 0.03 | 35 ± 3 | 40 ± 1 | 0.88 | 0.32 | 0.25 |

- **Interpretation:** tripling the amplitude cuts the stiffness by 3–4× and the loss jumps by about 10×, with a strong third harmonic. The boundaries stick and slip once the strain passes a threshold between 1 % and 3 %. This loss depends on amplitude and is nonlinear, the signature of grain-boundary and dislocation friction, rather than linear relaxation.
- **Consequence:** seismic strains are about 10⁻⁶, far below this threshold, so the seismically relevant boundary response is the linear, small-amplitude one. That points toward the rate-based boundary-sliding calculation I suggested: barriers and strain coupling for boundary sliding, extrapolated to small strain and low frequency.

**The aged Schottky cell is still lossy** (γ₀ = 0.02, after 1.5 ns of ageing; potential energy steady within 2 eV, so the cell is no longer changing):
- Q = 8.2 ± 1.4 at 200 GHz and 9.7 ± 3.0 at 50 GHz, with G′ of 142 and 131 GPa.
- The loss barely depends on frequency and persists after ageing. That fits the dynamic mechanism from step 3, not a Zener peak.
- The 20 GHz task is still running.

**Housekeeping:**
- **Watcher fix:** `sweep_watch.sh` flagged the bicrystal 50 GHz run as incomplete because the run overshot its step target (1.30 M of 1.25 M steps). A run now counts as complete when its last step reaches or passes the target. I ran the analysis for that run by hand.
- **Case file:** the γ₀ = 0.03 bicrystal now has its own key, `5000 bicrystal g3`, so it no longer mixes with the γ₀ = 0.01 data in the figures and causality check. I've rerun the collection.
- **Summary doc:** §3.1 of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` now includes these results.

The choice from before still stands, and the bicrystal result makes option 2 more attractive:
1. Test the dynamic vacancy-loss mechanism: does the excess loss scale with temperature like the jump rate?
2. Apply the rate approach to grain-boundary sliding in the bicrystal: barriers, strain coupling, and the small-amplitude loss extrapolated to low frequency.

```
def parse_lockin(root, mode=None):
    rows = []
    fn = f'{root}/lockin_results.txt'
    if not os.path.exists(fn):
        return rows
    for line in open(fn):
        if 'FAILED' in line or 'INCOMPLETE' in line:
            continue
        if mode and line.split()[1] != mode:
            continue
        m = re.search(r"f=" + num + r" Hz cycles=" + num, line)
        a = re.search(r"[GM]' = " + num + r" ± " + num + r" GPa; [GM]'' = " + num + r" ± " + num + " GPa", line)
        if not (m and a):
            continue
        h = re.search(r"\|σ2/σ1\| = " + num + r" \|σ3/σ1\| = " + num, line)
        d = line.split()[0]
        st = f'{root}/{d}/stress.dat'
        Tm = np.nan
        if os.path.exists(st):
            try:
                Tm = np.loadtxt(st, comments='#', usecols=7).mean()
            except Exception:
                pass
        Mp, Mpp = float(a.group(1)), float(a.group(3))
        ph = 2 * np.pi * float(m.group(1)) * LAG                          # Eq. 10
        Mp, Mpp = Mp * np.cos(ph) - Mpp * np.sin(ph), Mpp * np.cos(ph) + Mp * np.sin(ph)
        rows.append(dict(dir=d, f=float(m.group(1)), cyc=float(m.group(2)), Mp=Mp, dMp=float(a.group(2)),
                         Mpp=Mpp, dMpp=float(a.group(4)), h2=float(h.group(1)) if h else np.nan,
                         h3=float(h.group(2)) if h else np.nan, Tmean=Tm))
    rows.sort(key=lambda r: r['f'])
    return rows

def slope_estimates(rows, fmax_fit):
```

### Claude (10-08 17:19)

All the V9.6397 sweeps have finished. One correction to what I told you earlier, and two notable results:

**Correction:** the V9.6397 crystal Q values I quoted before were raw lock-in numbers, without the one-step lag correction that the collection script normally applies. Corrected values are lower:

| 3000 K cell | 20 GHz | 50 GHz | 0.2 THz | 1 THz |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| perfect B1 | — | — | > 200 | ≈ 100 (not 300) |
| Schottky | > 400 | ≈ 700 | ≈ 200 | ≈ 80 |

The 3000 K Schottky cell is nearly lossless at low frequency, unlike the 5000 K Schottky cell at V6.6868 (Q ≈ 10). That fits a dynamic defect loss tied to the jump rate, which is far lower at 3000 K.

**Result 1: the 3000 K bicrystal boundaries slide freely.**
- **Measurements:** at 0.2 THz and below, G′ ≈ 0, and the effective viscosity is 7.8 mPa s at both 20 and 50 GHz.
- **The cell has not melted:** Bragg peaks remain strong and atoms move only about 3 Å in 1.3 ns. The excess energy corresponds to a boundary energy of about 2.9 J/m². The two boundaries simply take all the shear, like viscous films.
- **Friction coefficient:** β ≈ 2.3×10⁶ Pa·s/m.
- **Contrast with V6.6868:** there, at 5000 K, the boundaries stay locked at 1 % strain and slide only at 3 %. Compression strongly suppresses grain-boundary sliding.
- **For real grains:** with this β, a 1 mm grain would relax within about 20 ns. Such boundaries would be fully relaxed, not dissipating, at seismic frequencies.

**Result 2: the 4000 K liquid crystallized under shear.** The suspect 50 GHz point came from a run in which the liquid crystallized at strain amplitude 0.32 within the first 70 ps (energy fell 0.38 eV/atom). Runs at the other periods stayed liquid. I've marked that point FAILED so the collection skips it. This task saved its trajectory, so the shear-induced crystallization can be animated from `4000K/FreqSweep/P20ps_r0/traj.lammpstrj`.

**Melt-grown crystals:** G′ ≈ 88–105 GPa at 3000 K and 95–115 GPa at 2000 K, rising with frequency, with Q ≈ 9–30. That is softer and lossier than the perfect crystal (120 GPa), consistent with grain boundaries that partly relax.

I've updated `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` §4 with all of this. The full V9.6397 collection will run automatically when the last Green–Kubo runs finish, in about 3 hours.

The choice between the next two steps is still open: testing the dynamic vacancy-loss mechanism, or applying the rate approach to grain-boundary sliding. Today's bicrystal results make the second one more compelling, especially comparing the two pressures.

### Claude (10-08 17:35)

I've started on grain-boundary sliding with job 4263056, six runs on a P100 at about 3.3 hours each, in `MgO/4096atoms/GBslide`. Two are running and the rest will follow.

**Step 1: measure sliding friction without applying any strain.**
- **Runs:** the Σ5 bicrystal at both volumes and three temperatures each: V6.6868 at 4000, 4500 and 5000 K, and V9.6397 at 2000, 2500 and 3000 K. Each has 20 ps of equilibration, then 300 ps with no thermostat, so the thermostat can't add friction of its own.
- **Measurement:** I track the relative sliding of the two grain interiors (900 and 910 atoms, staying 8 Å away from each boundary). The script `tools/gb_grains.py` locates each boundary from the atom density profile, since the boundaries had drifted by about 4 Å at V9.6397.
- **Theory:** the relative displacement u diffuses with D_u = kT/(2βA), where β is the friction per unit boundary area and A is the boundary area. This gives the small-amplitude friction directly, which is what matters at seismic strains of about 10⁻⁶. The sweeps, by contrast, measured either nonlinear stick-slip (γ₀ = 0.03) or locked boundaries (γ₀ = 0.01 at V6.6868).
- **Output:** across three temperatures at each volume, β(T) gives:
  - its own Arrhenius test of the single-activation-energy idea;
  - the pressure dependence. The sweeps already suggest boundaries locked at 140 GPa and sliding freely at 18 GPa.
- **Analysis tool:** `tools/gb_slide.py` reports D_u and β for sliding along x (perpendicular to the tilt axis) and y (along it). It also gives the cell's equivalent viscosity and flags locked boundaries, where u stops growing, with a lower bound on β.

**Next steps** (TODO item T18):
2. **Static sliding barriers:** the energy of the boundary against rigid sliding, with ASE and DeePMD, plus NEB of the elementary sliding or coupled boundary-migration step at both volumes. This gives barriers, attempt frequencies and strain coupling, as for the vacancies.
3. **Grain-scale model:** combine β(T, p) with grain size in the standard elastically accommodated sliding model, relaxation time τ ≈ βd/G. That predicts Q⁻¹ at seismic frequencies for mantle grain sizes, checked against our direct sweeps where they overlap.

Separately, the full V9.6397 collection still runs automatically once its last Green–Kubo jobs finish.

```
import argparse, os, sys

from ase.build import bulk

calc = DP(model=a.model)
A = (8 * a.vatom) ** (1 / 3)
at = bulk('MgO', 'rocksalt', a=A, cubic=True).repeat((a.n,) * 3); at.calc = calc
p = -at.get_stress()[:3].mean() * EV_A3_GPA
C = elastic(at, calc); C11, C12, C44 = C[0, 0], C[0, 1], C[3, 3]
K = (C11 + 2 * C12) / 3; GV = (C11 - C12 + 3 * C44) / 5; GR = 5 / (4 / (C11 - C12) + 3 / C44); GH = (GV + GR) / 2
nu = (3 * K - 2 * GH) / (2 * (3 * K + GH))
print(f'V = {a.vatom} A^3/atom, a = {A:.6f} A, static p = {p:.1f} GPa')
```

### Claude (10-08 18:00)

The grain-scale model is ready (`tools/gb_grain_model.py`) and waiting for the friction and diffusion numbers from the sliding runs, which take about 3 hours (job 4264732).

**Model.** It works per grain size d, using two inputs measured in MD at the actual temperature and volume: the boundary friction β and the boundary diffusivity δD_b. No temperature extrapolation is needed; only length and frequency are extrapolated.
- **Elastically accommodated sliding** (Zener; Raj and Ashby): one relaxation, with time τ ≈ βd/G.
  - The relaxation strength comes from Zener's spherical-grain estimate. With these Poisson ratios it gives Δ_J ≈ 0.6–0.7, meaning the polycrystal's shear modulus relaxes by about 40 %.
- **Diffusion-accommodated sliding (Coble creep):** a steady viscosity in series, η_C = kT·d³/(3·A_C·Ω·δD_eff). Here δD_eff combines the Mg and O boundary diffusivities, so the slower species controls.
- **Output:** Q⁻¹(ω) for d from 1 μm to 1 cm at each temperature and volume, plus the peak frequency, Q⁻¹ at 1 Hz, and a check against the bicrystal sweeps. The check treats the MD cell as a viscous element in series with the grains, with viscosity η_cell = βL_z/2.
- **Caveat:** the geometric factors are of order one, so the results are order-of-magnitude estimates.

**Inputs prepared:**
- **Boundary diffusion:** I restarted the sliding runs (job 4264732) so they also record the Mg and O mean-square displacements. Diffusion happens only in the two boundary layers, so δD_b = (cell MSD slope)·L_z/8, which doesn't depend on the boundary width. The first submission was cancelled after a few minutes and moved aside.
- **Elastic constants** (static, DeePMD):

  | volume | C₁₁ | C₁₂ | C₄₄ | Hill G | ν |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | V6.6868 | 1287 | 267 | 218 | 308 | 0.28 |
  | V9.6397 | 286 | 92 | 147 | 125 | 0.19 |

  Softened to temperature with the measured C₄₄ ratio, this gives G ≈ 240–250 GPa at 5000 K and about 100 GPa at 3000 K. That matches the melt-grown sweeps (258 and 88–105 GPa).
- **Model test:** I ran the script on the friction from the large-amplitude sweep at V9.6397. If boundaries were that free, a 1 mm grain would relax at about 6 MHz, giving only Q⁻¹ ≈ 10⁻⁷ at 1 Hz through this channel. Coble creep would then govern seismic loss. The real small-amplitude friction from the new runs will set this properly.

**Something conveniently close to the mantle:** V6.6868 at 4000 K is about 140 GPa, near core–mantle-boundary conditions, and V9.6397 at 2000 K is about 18 GPa at transition-zone temperatures. The model therefore needs no temperature extrapolation for these two cases.
