### Claude (10-08 18:30)

The aged Schottky series at V6.6868 and 5000 K is complete. The 20 GHz run, the last one, ran 18 cycles with steady potential energy.

| f | G′ (GPa) | G″ (GPa) | Q | η′ (Pa s) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 200 GHz | 142 ± 4 | 17 ± 3 | 8.2 | 0.014 |
| 50 GHz | 131 ± 3 | 13 ± 4 | 9.7 | 0.043 |
| 20 GHz | 141 ± 6 | 38 ± 9 | 3.7 | 0.30 |

The loss rises toward low frequency: G″ goes roughly as ω⁻¹ between 50 and 20 GHz, while G′ stays flat. That's the start of a slow, viscous-like process below 20 GHz, most likely stress relaxation by vacancy diffusion across the cell. Orientational (Zener) relaxation is already ruled out. I've added this to §3.1 of the summary.

The grain-boundary sliding runs are still going, about 2.5 hours left. When they finish, I'll feed them into the grain-scale model.

```
| 3000 Schottky | 3007 | 17.8 | 1500 | 30.1 | 1.30 ± 0.04 | 0.95 ± 0.04 | 4.44 ± 0.13 | 1.28 ± 0.04 | 0.043 |
| 3000 bicrystal | 3024 | 16.9 | 1500 | 52.9 | 1.00 ± 0.10 | 0.57 ± 0.03 | 3.00 ± 0.10 | 0.96 ± 0.08 | 0.019 |

Rows 3000, 2000, 3000 B1 ref, 3000 Schottky, 3000 bicrystal are **crystalline**. There, $G(0)=V\langle\delta\sigma^2\rangle/k_BT$ is only the fluctuation part of the modulus (the Born term is missing), and $\int G\,dt$ is the low-frequency loss from phonon damping and defects ($G''\approx\omega\int G\,dt$), not a viscosity. The elastic $G'$ of the solid comes from the sweeps.

| T (K) | $\eta(t_c)$ at $t_c$ = 0.5, 1, 2, 3 ps (mPa s) | $\zeta(t_c)$ at the same $t_c$ (mPa s) |
```

```
### 2a. Causality (local Kramers–Kronig) check, crystal sweeps (Eq. 15)

$dM'/d\ln\omega$ between adjacent frequencies against $(2/\pi)\langle M''\rangle$. Agreement means the measured loss is a linear, causal material response. Expected failures: slab resonance (bicrystal, Eq. 13), heated 5 THz points (†), and drifting (unaged) cells.
```

### Claude (10-08 21:13)

The V9.6397 series is complete and collected in `results_V9.6397/`. I've sent you its `fig_vs_T`.

**Liquid viscosity at the lower density:**

| T (K) | p (GPa) | η₀ Green–Kubo (mPa s) | η₀ sweeps (mPa s) | ζ₀ (mPa s) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6000 | 49.5 | 3.49 ± 0.08 | 3.46 ± 0.13 | 0.80 |
| 4000 (supercooled) | 37.8 | 7.99 ± 0.15 | 7.17 ± 0.23 | 1.51 |

- **Effect of pressure at 6000 K:** η₀ drops 5.2× from 175 GPa (18.3 mPa s) to 50 GPa (3.5 mPa s). The high-frequency shear modulus G∞ falls from 287 to 124 GPa, and the structural relaxation time from 0.064 to 0.028 ps.
- **The supercooled 4000 K liquid crystallizes when strained:** 3 of its 10 sweep runs crystallized partway through (shear at 20 ps; uniaxial at 0.2 and 100 ps). I've marked them FAILED, so its sweep η₀ rests on two points and reads about 10 % below Green–Kubo.
- **The 2000 K run also finished.** Its integral of 4.8 mPa s, like the other crystals', is phonon damping, not viscosity.
- **Bicrystal:** its Green–Kubo integral is still rising at 3 ps (1.0 → 2.6 mPa s), the slow sliding tail. The equilibrium sliding runs measure that directly; they have about 2 hours to go.

I've added this to §4 of the summary.

### Claude (10-08 21:20)

The sliding measurements and the first grain-scale model are done. At high pressure the boundary doesn't slide at all within our run time, while at low pressure it slides readily in one direction. I've sent you `fig_gb_sliding` and `fig_gb_Q_model`.

**Friction β of the Σ5 boundary**, from 300 ps runs with no applied strain:

| volume | T (K) | sliding perpendicular to tilt axis | sliding along tilt axis |
|---|---|---|---|
| V6.6868 (140–150 GPa) | 4000–5000 | locked: β ≥ 3–20×10⁹ Pa s/m | locked: β ≥ 3–20×10⁹ Pa s/m |
| V9.6397 (14–20 GPa) | 2000 | β = 5×10⁷ (±100 %) | locked: β ≥ 2×10⁹ |
| V9.6397 (14–20 GPa) | 2500–3000 | β = 5–8×10⁶ (±50–70 %) | locked: β ≥ 2×10⁹ |

- **High pressure locks the boundary:** at 140–150 GPa the grains move less than 0.3 Å in 300 ps, even at 5000 K. At 14–20 GPa they slide 1–6 Å, but only perpendicular to the tilt axis, the direction in which sliding is coupled to boundary migration. Along the tilt axis it stays locked, so the friction differs by at least 300× between the two directions.
- **Consistent with the sweeps:** at V9.6397 and 3000 K, the friction gives a cell viscosity of 27 mPa s. The 0.2 THz sweep gives 17 mPa s and the large-amplitude sweep 7.8 mPa s, the drop expected from stick-slip. At V6.6868 the bound predicts nearly elastic small-amplitude sweeps, which is what we measured.
- **Temperature dependence:** β falls about 10× from 2000 to 2500 K, then shows no clear change up to 3000 K. With one 300 ps run per temperature, that's too noisy to test the single-activation-energy idea; it would need longer runs or replicas.

**Grain-scale model** (sliding accommodated elastically, plus boundary-diffusion creep):
- **14–20 GPa, 2000–3000 K:**
  - **Peak frequency:** about 0.2–2 MHz for 1 mm grains.
  - **Loss at 1 Hz:** Q⁻¹ ≈ 10⁻⁷–10⁻⁶ for 1 mm grains and 10⁻⁶–10⁻⁵ for 1 cm.
  - **Meaning:** boundaries like this one would be fully relaxed at seismic frequencies, so they would lower the seismic shear modulus (by up to Zener's relaxation strength, about 0.75–0.96) rather than attenuate.
- **140–150 GPa, 4000–5000 K** (near core–mantle-boundary conditions):
  - The friction is only a lower bound, so the peak lies at or below about 0.5–10 kHz.
  - It would fall into the seismic band if the true friction is 10³–10⁵ times the bound, which MD can't rule out.
  - Settling this needs the static sliding barriers (T18 step 2: NEB of the coupled sliding step).

**Caveats:**
- Σ5 is a single special, low-energy boundary.
- The model's geometric factors are only order-one estimates.
- The boundary diffusion is an upper bound: the whole-cell MSD includes the grains' sliding motion. A boundary-only MSD would fix this.

I've added this as §5d of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md` (Eqs. 12–13) and updated TODO item T18.

**Suggested next step:** the static sliding barriers at both volumes using ASE and DeePMD, which is the only route to the actual friction at high pressure. It takes a few GPU-hours, the same scale as the vacancy NEB.

### Claude (10-09 04:47)

The static sliding calculations are running, four jobs in all.

**Jobs:**
- **As-built Σ5 boundary**, one job per volume (4281469 and 4281470).
- **MD-equilibrated boundaries, quenched to 0 K** (4281476 and 4281477): taken from the end of the 4000 K sliding run at V6.6868 and the 2000 K run at V9.6397.

**What each job calculates:**
1. The energy of rigidly sliding one grain against the other, along x (perpendicular to the tilt axis) and along y (the tilt axis). The grain interiors are held in place and the boundary region relaxes.
2. A climbing-image NEB, with all atoms free, along the easiest direction to the first equivalent position. The NEB can find shear-coupled boundary migration if that is the easier path.
3. The friction β predicted by transition-state theory for the same periodic cell, to compare directly with the β from the MD.

**First numbers, as-built boundary at V6.6868:** the energy profile for sliding along x varies by only about 0.3–1 eV for the whole cell, about 0.01–0.02 J/m² per boundary, and is jagged. A barrier that small should let the boundary slide freely in MD at 4000–5000 K, but the MD shows it locked. My guess is that the boundary rearranged during the MD into a more strongly bonded, locked structure, which the MD-start runs will test. The as-built cell at V9.6397 also starts slightly above its lowest-energy sliding position, so its initial alignment isn't optimal.

I'll report when all four jobs finish.

```
import numpy as np
from ase.io import read
f='/groups/carnegie_poc/rcohen/LAMMPS/MgO/4096atoms/GBslide/V6.6868_4000K/end.data'
at=read(f,format='lammps-data',atom_style='atomic',units='metal'); L=at.cell.lengths()
z = at.positions[:, 2] % L[2]
nb = 200; h, e = np.histogram(z, bins=nb, range=(0, L[2])); c = 0.5 * (e[1:] + e[:-1])
kern = np.exp(-0.5 * (np.arange(-15, 16) * L[2] / nb / 1.5) ** 2); kern /= kern.sum()
hs = np.real(np.fft.ifft(np.fft.fft(h) * np.fft.fft(np.r_[kern[15:], np.zeros(nb - 31), kern[:15]])))
b1 = c[np.argmin(hs)]; print('b1',b1)
L2=open(f).read().split('\n'); i=[k for k,l in enumerate(L2) if l.startswith('Atoms')][0]
zz=np.array([float(l.split()[4]) for l in L2[i+2:i+2+3920]])%L[2]
print('max |z_ase - z_file| sorted', np.abs(np.sort(z)-np.sort(zz)).max(), L2[i], L2[i+2])
EOF
```

### Claude (10-09 05:05)

The as-built boundary results are in, and the two MD-start runs have been resubmitted with the fixed boundary detection (jobs 4281615 and 4281616).

**As-built Σ5 boundary**, relaxed sliding energy per boundary:

| volume | perpendicular to tilt axis (x) | along tilt axis (y) |
|---|---|---|
| V6.6868 | maximum 0.018 J/m², minima every 1.19 Å | maximum 1.93 J/m² at a/2 |
| V9.6397 | about 0 for shifts up to ±2.4 Å, 1.1–1.5 J/m² beyond | maximum 0.99 J/m², local minimum at a/2 |

- **x sliding:**
  - At V6.6868 the minima are spaced at the boundary's natural repeat distance (a/√10 = 1.19 Å). The NEB barrier is only 0.36 eV for the whole cell (0.006 J/m²).
  - At V9.6397 the starting structure is not at its minimum, and it slides downhill with no barrier.
- **Sliding is strongly anisotropic** at both volumes: easy across the tilt axis, hard along it (1–2 J/m²). That matches the V9.6397 MD, where x slides and y is locked.

**The puzzle:** at V6.6868, the as-built barrier predicts nearly free sliding, with transition-state friction β ≈ 2.5×10⁵ Pa s/m. The MD at 4000–5000 K shows the boundary locked, with β ≥ 3×10⁹, at least 10⁴ times more. The likely explanation is that in the MD the boundary rearranged at high pressure and temperature into a different, interlocked structure. The MD-start calculations test exactly that. They begin from the boundary after 1.5 ns at 5000 K plus 300 ps at 4000 K (V6.6868), and after 300 ps at 2000 K (V9.6397), each quenched to 0 K.

### Claude (10-09 05:14)

The static sliding calculations are done. They explain why the boundary locks at high pressure, but they also show that the friction from the previous step can't be carried over to real grains.

**Relaxed sliding energy per boundary:**

| boundary | perpendicular to tilt axis (x) | along tilt axis (y) |
|---|---|---|
| 140 GPa, as built | ≤ 0.018 J/m² variation | 1.9 J/m² |
| 140 GPa, after MD at 5000 → 4000 K | 0.03–0.07 J/m² | 1.2 J/m² |
| 18 GPa, as built | flat for shifts up to ±2.4 Å, then 1.1–1.5 J/m² | 1.0 J/m² |
| 18 GPa, after MD at 2000 K | flat for shifts up to ±1.3 Å, then 0.6–1.35 J/m² | 1.0 J/m² |

1. **Sliding is strongly anisotropic** in every case: easy perpendicular to the tilt axis, about 1–2 J/m² along it. This matches the MD at 18 GPa, where x slid and y stayed locked.
2. **At 140 GPa the boundary rearranges during MD.** After MD, the energy variation along x is 3–4× larger than for the as-built boundary. For the whole periodic cell, that raises the sliding barrier from 0.36 eV to about 3–4 eV. At 4000–5000 K that allows only about 10⁸ jumps per second, so nothing happens in 300 ps, which explains the locking. The as-built boundary would have slid freely.
3. **At 18 GPa the large MD displacements weren't rigid sliding.** Rigid sliding is free only within ±1.3–2.4 Å and then costs about 70–95 eV per cell. Yet the grains moved about 6 Å at 3000 K, while both boundaries migrated about 10 Å. The motion beyond the flat region is boundary migration coupled to shear, which a rigid-sliding calculation can't represent.

**Consequence: the grain-scale model from the previous step is provisional.** In a small periodic cell, sliding is a collective event whose barrier grows with the boundary area. So the cell friction β, whether from MD or transition-state theory, is not a material constant. Real boundaries slide by moving local steps, whose barrier doesn't depend on the total area. What does carry over is the ideal sliding stress: about 1–2 GPa at 140 GPa, and several GPa beyond the flat region at 18 GPa. The NEB end-point choice in the script also picked wrong end states in two runs, so only the energy profiles are reliable; I've noted it to fix.

I've added this as §5e of `ANELASTICITY_RESULTS_SUMMARY.md`, flagged the model as provisional, and updated T18.

**Proposed next step: a size test.** I'd repeat the equilibrium sliding run at 18 GPa and 3000 K with 2× and 4× the boundary area (7840 and 15 680 atoms). If β stays the same, sliding happens locally and the friction carries over to real grains. If β grows with area, the mechanism is collective, and the next step would be a NEB of the coupled step with end states taken from the MD.

The cost is about 6 hours and 11 hours on one P100 each, roughly 17 P100-hours in total. Should I run it?

## User (10-09 08:32)

yes go ahead with the size test. We can do bigger systems.

### Claude (10-09 08:50)

The size-test runs are under way as job 4283403, four tasks on separate P100s, in `MgO/4096atoms/GBslide/<dir>`:

| run | boundary area | atoms | NVE length |
|---|---|---|---|
| 18 GPa (V9.6397), 3000 K | 2× (2×1) | 7840 | 300 ps |
| 18 GPa, 3000 K | 4× (2×2) | 15 680 | 300 ps |
| 18 GPa, 3000 K | 9× (3×3) | 35 280 | 250 ps |
| 140 GPa (V6.6868), 5000 K | 9× (3×3) | 35 280 | 200 ps |

Each starts with 10 ps of equilibration, and each grain interior contains 2000–9800 atoms.

**Why these four:**
- The three 18 GPa runs, together with the original 1× cell, test whether the friction β depends on boundary area.
  - If β stays roughly constant, sliding happens through local events, and the friction carries over to real grains.
  - If β grows roughly in proportion to area, sliding is a collective event of the whole small cell, and the grain model needs the step-motion (disconnection) mechanism instead.
- The 140 GPa run at 9× area checks whether the locked boundary starts sliding once local mechanisms have room to operate.

The 35 000-atom cells fit on one P100 (14.6 GB available). I'm checking their speed now. The largest runs should take about 20 hours, so the full result will arrive tomorrow. The 2× run should finish in about 5 hours, and I'll report it first.

```
---
name: anelasticity-project
description: MgO liquid viscosity/anelasticity project with LAMMPS+DeePMD oscillatory NEMD; where runs live and known analysis pitfalls
metadata:
  type: project
---
Started 2026-10-05. Goal: viscosity and anelasticity of high-P MgO (V=6.6868 Å³/atom, ~190 GPa at 8000 K), cooling liquid until it crystallizes; later modulus vs frequency (Gross 1953 relaxation-spectrum theory, PDF is scanned, no text layer).
Sample runs: /groups/carnegie_poc/rcohen/LAMMPS/MgO/4096atoms/V6.6868/4096atom/8000K/Viscosity/{x/R1A1/2267049 (uniaxial x wiggle), 54671682 (xz shear wiggle)}. fix deform wiggle + nvt/sllod, period 1 ps, GPU, ~/bin/lmp, deepmd_venv.
**Why:** user's jupytext notebooks (stressstrain*.py) assume 10 fs sampling, use raw xz tilt as strain, and omit 2π/strain amplitude in η; lock-in fit at the drive frequency is the reliable analysis.
**How to apply:** analyze with least-squares lock-in; uniaxial x gives longitudinal M=K+4/3G and ζ+4/3η, not shear η.
Restart docs (same convention as WSDA): /resnick/home/recohen/Claude/Anelasticity/ANELASTICITY_RESTART_<date>.md, REQUIREMENTS.md, ANELASTICITY_TODO.md, ANELASTICITY_DONE.md, ANELASTICITY_PLAN.md. Read latest restart first.
Methods guide (read before any new volume/material): /resnick/home/recohen/Claude/Anelasticity/ANELASTICITY_METHODS.md. Second volume V9.6397 started 2026-10-08 (runs in .../4096atoms/V9.6397/4096atom; RC's older NEMD runs in .../V9.6397/Viscosity, don't touch). ### Claude (10-09 14:59)
```

I've written the restart document, `Anelasticity/ANELASTICITY_RESTART_2026-10-09.md`, and updated `ANELASTICITY_DONE.md` and the project memory.

I took "restarts" to mean the restart document. If you meant restart files for the running LAMMPS jobs, they aren't needed: a timed-out run still writes its final state and keeps all its sliding data, so the analysis works on a partial run.

**What the restart covers:**
- **§0, what to check first:** the three remaining size-test runs (job 4283403: 18 GPa at 4× and 9× boundary area, 140 GPa at 9×). It gives the exact analysis command in case the background waiter is gone, and how to read the outcome.
- **§1, what was done on 10-08 and 10-09:**
  - documentation and collection-script changes;
  - fixes (the job watcher, and the 4000 K liquid runs that crystallized and are now marked FAILED);
  - OCTP;
  - the vacancy work: hop statistics, NEB, transition-state theory, elastic dipoles;
  - the grain-boundary work: sliding friction, γ-lines, the grain model;
  - every new tool by name;
  - the known bug in the NEB end-point choice.
- **§2, next steps:** in order, depending on how the size test comes out.

**Interim size-test result:** the 2× boundary-area run has finished. Its friction perpendicular to the tilt axis is β = 3.0×10⁶ Pa s/m, against 8×10⁶ in the 1× cell, so β doesn't grow with area. That points to a local sliding mechanism, which would mean the friction carries over to real grains. The 4× and 9× runs will confirm or overturn this.

### Claude (10-09 15:00)

I've sent both figures. PDF versions are in `Anelasticity/results/`.

- **`fig_gb_sliding`:**
  - **Left:** how far the two grains drift apart over time. Curves that keep rising mean the boundary slides: 18 GPa, perpendicular to the tilt axis. Curves that level off below 0.05 Å² mean it is locked: along the tilt axis, and in every direction at 140 GPa.
  - **Right:** the boundary friction β against 1/T. Filled symbols are measured values; open symbols with arrows are lower bounds for locked directions.
  - These are the 1× cells only; the size-test points will be added when those runs finish.
- **`fig_gb_Q_model`:** the provisional grain-scale Q⁻¹ against frequency, for grain sizes from 1 µm to 1 cm.
  - The 140 GPa panel is labelled as lower bounds: the loss peaks lie at or below the frequencies shown.

### Claude (10-09 15:01)

I've sent you `fig_effective_viscosity` for V9.6397. The PDF is in `Anelasticity/results_V9.6397/`.

- **Crystals:** the melt-grown crystals at 2000 and 3000 K and the perfect and Schottky cells all follow the elastic-solid line, G′/ω with G′ ≈ 91 GPa. At 0.2 THz their |η*| is about 70–100 mPa s, and their flow-viscosity lower bounds are 1.5–20 Pa s.
- **Bicrystal (grey):** this stands out. From 20 to 200 GHz its |η*| is about 6–9 mPa s, flat, at the level of the 4000 K liquid. The boundaries slide like viscous films, so the cell as a whole behaves like a liquid. It drops at 5 THz because of the slab resonance.
- **Caveat:** in this volume's sweep the bicrystal's 20 and 50 GHz points used a larger strain amplitude (γ₀ = 0.03, nonlinear) than its 0.2–5 THz points (γ₀ = 0.01), and they are plotted as one line. The equilibrium sliding runs give a linear cell viscosity of about 27 mPa s, so the low-frequency points underestimate it.
- **Liquids:** 6000 K plateaus at η₀ = 3.5 mPa s and 4000 K at 8.0 mPa s. Sweeps and Green–Kubo agree.

### Claude (10-09 15:02)

I've sent `fig_kk` for both volumes, V6.6868 first and V9.6397 second. Each shows G′ and M′ against frequency (dots), with the dashed curve the stiffness predicted from the measured loss by the causality (Kramers–Kronig) relation, Eq. 15. The full tables are in §2a of each `results_all.md`.

**V6.6868 (5000 and 4000 K):** the melt-grown and perfect crystals agree within 1σ from 0.2 to 5 THz. The only failures are the bicrystal near its slab resonance and the heated 5 THz uniaxial points.

**V9.6397 (2000 and 3000 K):** agreement is weaker, as listed below (σ is the uncertainty of the difference).

| case | 0.2–1 THz | 1–5 THz |
|---|---|---|
| melt-grown, shear | 0.7–2.5σ | 2.8–4.7σ |
| melt-grown, uniaxial | 2.0–2.3σ | 1.1σ (3000 K), 15σ (2000 K) |
| perfect, shear | 0.3σ | 18σ |
| perfect, uniaxial | 1.4σ | 8σ |
| Schottky, shear | 3.5σ | 1.1σ |
| Schottky, uniaxial | 1.3σ | 0.9σ |

- **Schottky shear at 20–200 GHz** agrees within 0.6–1.3σ.
- **The 5 THz points account for most failures.** Here the softer, lower-pressure crystal is closer to its phonon frequencies, so the loss changes too quickly with frequency for Eq. 15's local form. These points also heat during the run.
- **Bicrystal, shear, fails everywhere (3.5–12σ),** as expected: G′ ≈ 0 with nonlinear sliding at low frequency, and the slab resonance near 1 THz. A freely sliding boundary isn't a small linear perturbation of the crystal.

**Conclusion:** at both volumes, the bulk-crystal loss at 0.2–1 THz is a genuine linear, causal material response. The 5 THz points and the bicrystal are where the local causality relation can't be applied.
